[Japanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_language "Japanese language"): 京城, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Japanese "Romanization of Japanese"):_Keijō_; [Korean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_language "Korean language"):경성; [RR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revised_Romanization_of_Korean "Revised Romanization of Korean"):_Gyeongseong_; [MR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McCune%E2%80%93Reischauer "McCune–Reischauer"):_Kyŏngsŏng_<br>2. According to Korean Christians[&#8202;](#ref-back-Wi_Jo_Kang_1997_62-0)[\[5\]](#refnote-Wi_Jo_Kang_1997_62)\[ _[need quotation to verify](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability "Wikipedia:Verifiability")_\] |  |  |  |  |  |

From 1910 to 1945, [Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korea "Korea") was ruled by the [Empire of Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Japan "Empire of Japan") as a colony under the name **Chōsen** (朝鮮), the Japanese reading of " [Joseon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseon "Joseon")".[&#8202;](#ref-back-10-0)[\[a\]](#refnote-10)

Japan first took Korea into its sphere of influence during the late 1800s. Both Korea ( [Joseon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseon "Joseon")) and Japan had been under policies of [isolationism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isolationism "Isolationism"), with Joseon being a [tributary state of Qing China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tributary_system_of_China "Tributary system of China"). However, in 1854, [Japan was forcibly opened by the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perry_Expedition "Perry Expedition"). It then rapidly modernized under the [Meiji Restoration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meiji_Restoration "Meiji Restoration"), while Joseon continued to resist foreign attempts to open it up. Japan eventually succeeded [in forcefully opening Joseon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganghwa_Island_incident "Ganghwa Island incident") with the unequal [Japan–Korea Treaty of 1876](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan%E2%80%93Korea_Treaty_of_1876 "Japan–Korea Treaty of 1876"). Afterwards, Japan embarked on a decades-long process of defeating its local rivals, securing alliances with Western powers, and asserting its influence in Korea. Japan [assassinated the defiant Korean queen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Empress_Myeongseong "Assassination of Empress Myeongseong") and intervened in the [Donghak Peasant Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donghak_Peasant_Revolution "Donghak Peasant Revolution").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:03-0)[\[10\]](#refnote-:03)[&#8202;](#ref-back-12-0)[\[11\]](#refnote-12) After Japan defeated China in the 1894–1895 [First Sino–Japanese War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Sino-Japanese_War "First Sino-Japanese War"), Joseon became nominally independent and declared the short-lived [Korean Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Empire "Korean Empire"). Japan defeated Russia in the 1904–1905 [Russo-Japanese War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Japanese_War "Russo-Japanese War"), making it the sole regional power.

Japan acted quickly to fully absorb Korea. It first made Korea a [protectorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectorate "Protectorate") under the [Japan–Korea Treaty of 1905](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan%E2%80%93Korea_Treaty_of_1905 "Japan–Korea Treaty of 1905"), and [ruled the country indirectly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indirect_rule "Indirect rule") through the [Japanese Resident-General of Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_Resident-General_of_Korea "Japanese Resident-General of Korea"). After forcing Emperor [Gojong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gojong_of_Korea "Gojong of Korea") to abdicate in 1907, Japan formally annexed Korea with the [Japan–Korea Treaty of 1910](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan%E2%80%93Korea_Treaty_of_1910 "Japan–Korea Treaty of 1910"). For decades it administered the territory by its appointed [Governor-General of Chōsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_Ch%C5%8Dsen "Governor-General of Chōsen"), who was based in [Keijō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keij%C5%8D "Keijō") ( [Seoul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seoul "Seoul")). Japan made sweeping changes in Korea. Under the pretext of the racial theory known as _[Nissen dōsoron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nissen_d%C5%8Dsoron "Nissen dōsoron")_, it began a process of [Japanization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanization "Japanization"), eventually [functionally banning the use of Korean names](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C5%8Dshi-kaimei "Sōshi-kaimei") and the Korean language altogether. Its forces transported tens of thousands of cultural artifacts to Japan. Hundreds of historic buildings, such as the [Gyeongbokgung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gyeongbokgung "Gyeongbokgung") and [Deoksugung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deoksugung "Deoksugung") palaces, were either partially or completely demolished.

Japan built infrastructure and industry to develop the colony. It directed the construction of railways, ports, and roads, although in numerous cases, workers were subjected to extremely poor working circumstances and discriminatory pay. While Korea's economy grew under Japan, scholars argue that many of the infrastructure projects were designed to extract resources from the peninsula, and not to benefit its people. In addition, Koreans faced heavy taxation, with rates in some cases exceeding 50%.[&#8202;](#ref-back-economic-0)[\[12\]](#refnote-economic)[&#8202;](#ref-back-shocks-0)[\[13\]](#refnote-shocks) Most of Korea's infrastructure built during this time was destroyed during the 1950–1953 [Korean War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_War "Korean War").[&#8202;](#ref-back-15-0)[\[14\]](#refnote-15)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERobinson2007119–120-0)[\[15\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERobinson2007119–120)[&#8202;](#ref-back-17-0)[\[16\]](#refnote-17)[&#8202;](#ref-back-18-0)[\[17\]](#refnote-18)

These conditions led to the birth of the [Korean independence movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_independence_movement "Korean independence movement"), which acted both politically and [militantly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_militant_Korean_independence_activist_organizations "List of militant Korean independence activist organizations"), sometimes within the Japanese Empire, but mostly from outside of it. Koreans were subjected to a number of mass murders, including the [Gando massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gando_massacre "Gando massacre"), [Kantō Massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kant%C5%8D_Massacre "Kantō Massacre"), [Jeamni massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeamni_massacre "Jeamni massacre"), and [Shinano River incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shinano_River_incident "Shinano River incident"). Beginning in 1939 and during [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II "World War II"), Japan mobilized around 5.4million Koreans to support its war effort. Many were moved forcefully from their homes, and set to work in generally extremely poor working conditions. Many women and girls were forced into sexual slavery as " [comfort women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comfort_women "Comfort women")" to Japanese soldiers. In addition to these atrocities, millions of Koreans fell victim to various [Japanese war crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_war_crimes "Japanese war crimes") throughout the colonial period, including forced labor, human experimentation, and systemic starvation. Ultimately, the nature of Japanese imperial rule and its widespread war crimes led to immense suffering and loss of life, leaving a deep and lasting scar on the nation's history. After the [surrender of Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surrender_of_Japan "Surrender of Japan") at the end of the war, Korea was liberated from Japanese colonial rule, but [was immediately divided](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Division_of_Korea "Division of Korea") into occupation zones under the [rule of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Civil_Administration "Soviet Civil Administration") and [of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Army_Military_Government_in_Korea "United States Army Military Government in Korea"), led to the foundations of both [North](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Korea "North Korea") and [South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Korea "South Korea") in 1948.

The legacy of Japanese colonization has been hotly contested, and it continues to be extremely controversial. There is a significant range of opinions in both South Korea and Japan, and historical topics regularly cause diplomatic issues. Within South Korea, a particular focus is the role of the numerous ethnic [Korean collaborators with Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_collaborators_with_the_Empire_of_Japan "Korean collaborators with the Empire of Japan"). They have been variously punished or left alone. This controversy is exemplified in the legacy of [Park Chung Hee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Park_Chung_Hee "Park Chung Hee"), South Korea's most influential and controversial president. He collaborated with the Japanese military and continued to praise it even after the colonial period. Until 1965, South Korea and Japan had no functional diplomatic relations, until they signed the [Treaty on Basic Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_on_Basic_Relations_between_Japan_and_the_Republic_of_Korea "Treaty on Basic Relations between Japan and the Republic of Korea"). It declared "already [null and void](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Null_and_void "Null and void")"[&#8202;](#ref-back-19-0)[\[18\]](#refnote-19) all treaties made between the Empires of Japan and Korea on or before 22 August 1910.[&#8202;](#ref-back-AnnexTreaty-0)[\[19\]](#refnote-AnnexTreaty) Despite this, [relations between Japan and South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan%E2%80%93South_Korea_relations "Japan–South Korea relations") have oscillated between warmer and cooler periods, often due to conflicts over the historiography of this era.

## Terminology

During the period of Japanese colonial rule, Korea was officially known as Chōsen (朝鮮),[&#8202;](#ref-back-WaPo-0)[\[7\]](#refnote-WaPo)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Brooklyn-0)[\[8\]](#refnote-Brooklyn)[&#8202;](#ref-back-LA_Times-0)[\[9\]](#refnote-LA_Times) although the former name continued to be used internationally.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Reno-0)[\[20\]](#refnote-Reno)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Montreal-0)[\[21\]](#refnote-Montreal)

In South Korea, the period is usually described as the "Imperial Japanese compulsive occupation period" ( [Korean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_language "Korean language"):일제강점기; [Hanja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanja "Hanja"):日帝强占期; [RR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revised_Romanization_of_Korean "Revised Romanization of Korean"):_Ilje Gangjeomgi_).[&#8202;](#ref-back-23-0)[\[22\]](#refnote-23) Other terms, although often considered obsolete, include "Japanese Imperial Period" (일제시대;日帝時代;_Ilje Sidae_),[&#8202;](#ref-back-24-0)[\[23\]](#refnote-24) "The dark Japanese Imperial Period" (일제암흑기;日帝暗黑期;_Ilje Amheukgi_),[&#8202;](#ref-back-25-0)[\[24\]](#refnote-25) and " [Wae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wa_(name_of_Japan) "Wa (name of Japan)") (Japanese) administration period" (왜정시대;倭政時代;_Wae-jeong Sidae_).[&#8202;](#ref-back-26-0)[\[25\]](#refnote-26)

In Japan, the term "Chōsen of the Japanese-Governed Period" (日本統治時代の朝鮮, _Nippon Tōchi-jidai no Chōsen_) has been used.

## Background

### Political turmoil in Korea

#### Japan–Korea Treaty of 1876

Main article: [Japan–Korea Treaty of 1876](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan%E2%80%93Korea_Treaty_of_1876 "Japan–Korea Treaty of 1876")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/f/f5/Soldiers_from_the_Un%27y%C5%8D_attacking_the_Yeongjong_castle_on_a_Korean_island_%28woodblock_print%2C_1876%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Soldiers_from_the_Un'y%C5%8D_attacking_the_Yeongjong_castle_on_a_Korean_island_(woodblock_print,_1876).jpg) Japanese marines landing from the _Unyo_ at [Yeongjong Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yeongjong_Island "Yeongjong Island") which is near Ganghwa

On 27 February 1876, the [Japan–Korea Treaty of 1876](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan%E2%80%93Korea_Treaty_of_1876 "Japan–Korea Treaty of 1876") was signed. It was designed to open up Korea to Japanese trade, and the rights granted to Japan under the treaty were similar to those granted Western powers in Japan following the visit of [Commodore Perry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_C._Perry "Matthew C. Perry") in 1854.[&#8202;](#ref-back-autogenerated1-0)[\[26\]](#refnote-autogenerated1) The treaty ended [Chinese suzerainty over Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tributary_system_of_China "Tributary system of China"), forced opening of three Korean ports to Japanese trade, granted [extraterritorial rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extraterritoriality "Extraterritoriality") to Japanese citizens, and was an [unequal treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unequal_treaty "Unequal treaty") signed under duress ( [gunboat diplomacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gunboat_diplomacy "Gunboat diplomacy")) of the [Ganghwa Island incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganghwa_Island_incident "Ganghwa Island incident") of 1875.[&#8202;](#ref-back-autogenerated1-1)[\[26\]](#refnote-autogenerated1)

#### Imo Incident

Main articles: [Imo Incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imo_Incident "Imo Incident") and [Japan–Korea Treaty of 1882](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan%E2%80%93Korea_Treaty_of_1882 "Japan–Korea Treaty of 1882")

The regent [Daewongun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daewongun "Daewongun"), who remained opposed to any concessions to Japan or the West, helped organize the Mutiny of 1882, an anti-Japanese outbreak against [Queen Min](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Min "Queen Min") and her allies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Marius_B._Jansen-0)[\[27\]](#refnote-Marius_B._Jansen) Motivated by resentment of the preferential treatment given to newly trained troops, the Daewongun's forces, or "old military", killed a Japanese training cadre, and attacked the Japanese [legation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legation "Legation").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Marius_B._Jansen-1)[\[27\]](#refnote-Marius_B._Jansen) Japanese diplomats,[&#8202;](#ref-back-故広戸一等巡査外五名靖国神社ヘ合祀p.6_left-0)[\[28\]](#refnote-故広戸一等巡査外五名靖国神社ヘ合祀p.6_left) policemen,[&#8202;](#ref-back-故広戸一等巡査外五名靖国神社ヘ合祀p.2_left-0)[\[29\]](#refnote-故広戸一等巡査外五名靖国神社ヘ合祀p.2_left) students,[&#8202;](#ref-back-朝鮮国ニ於テ戦死セシ語学生靖国神社ヘ合祀ヲ許サス-0)[\[30\]](#refnote-朝鮮国ニ於テ戦死セシ語学生靖国神社ヘ合祀ヲ許サス) and some Min clan members were also killed during the incident. The Daewongun was briefly restored to power, only to be forcibly taken to China by Chinese troops dispatched to Seoul to prevent further disorder.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Marius_B._Jansen-2)[\[27\]](#refnote-Marius_B._Jansen)

In August 1882, the [Japan–Korea Treaty of 1882](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan%E2%80%93Korea_Treaty_of_1882 "Japan–Korea Treaty of 1882") indemnified the families of the Japanese victims, paid reparations to the Japanese government in the amount of 500,000 yen, and allowed a company of Japanese guards to be stationed at the Japanese legation in Seoul.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Marius_B._Jansen-3)[\[27\]](#refnote-Marius_B._Jansen)

#### Kapsin Coup

Main articles: [Kapsin Coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kapsin_Coup "Kapsin Coup") and [Gaehwa Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaehwa_Party "Gaehwa Party")

The struggle between the Heungseon Daewongun's followers and those of Queen Min was further complicated by competition from a Korean independence faction known as the Progressive Party ( _Gaehwa-dang_), as well as the Conservative faction. While the former sought Japan's support, the latter sought China's support.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Marius_B._Jansen-4)[\[27\]](#refnote-Marius_B._Jansen)

On 4 December 1884, the Progressive Party, assisted by the Japanese, attempted the [Kapsin Coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kapsin_Coup "Kapsin Coup"), in which they attempted to maintain Gojong but replace the government with a pro-Japanese one. They also wished to liberate Korea from Chinese suzerainty.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Marius_B._Jansen-5)[\[27\]](#refnote-Marius_B._Jansen) However, this proved short-lived, as conservative Korean officials requested the help of Chinese forces stationed in Korea.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Marius_B._Jansen-6)[\[27\]](#refnote-Marius_B._Jansen) The coup was put down by Chinese troops, and a Korean mob killed both Japanese officers and Japanese residents in retaliation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Marius_B._Jansen-7)[\[27\]](#refnote-Marius_B._Jansen) Some leaders of the Progressive Party, including [Kim Okkyun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Okkyun "Kim Okkyun"), fled to Japan, while others were executed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Marius_B._Jansen-8)[\[27\]](#refnote-Marius_B._Jansen) For the next 10 years, Japanese expansion into the Korean economy was approximated only by the efforts of [tsarist Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire "Russian Empire"), but eventually would be annexed by Japan in 1910 (See Prelude to annexation).

#### Donghak Revolution and First Sino-Japanese War

Main articles: [Donghak Peasant Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donghak_Peasant_Revolution "Donghak Peasant Revolution") and [First Sino-Japanese War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Sino-Japanese_War "First Sino-Japanese War")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/First_Sino-Japanese_War.svg/960px-First_Sino-Japanese_War.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:First_Sino-Japanese_War.svg) Major battles and troop movements during the [First Sino-Japanese War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Sino-Japanese_War "First Sino-Japanese War")

The outbreak of the [Donghak Peasant Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donghak_Peasant_Revolution "Donghak Peasant Revolution") in 1894 provided a seminal pretext for direct military intervention by Japan in the affairs of Korea. In April 1894, Joseon asked for Chinese assistance in ending the revolt. In response, Japanese leaders, citing a violation of the [Convention of Tientsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_of_Tientsin "Convention of Tientsin") as a pretext, decided upon military intervention to challenge China. On 3 May 1894, 1,500 Qing forces appeared in [Incheon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incheon "Incheon"). On 23 July 1894, Japan attacked Seoul in defiance of the Korean government's demand for withdrawal, and then occupied it and started the Sino-Japanese War. Japan won the [First Sino-Japanese War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Sino-Japanese_War "First Sino-Japanese War"), and China signed the [Treaty of Shimonoseki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Shimonoseki "Treaty of Shimonoseki") in 1895. Among its many stipulations, the treaty recognized "the full and complete independence and autonomy of Korea", thus ending Joseon's [tributary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tributary_state "Tributary state") relationship with Qing, leading to the proclamation of the full independence of Joseon in 1895. At the same time, Japan suppressed the peasant revolt with Korean government forces.

#### Assassination of Queen Min

Main article: [Assassination of Empress Myeongseong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Empress_Myeongseong "Assassination of Empress Myeongseong")

The Japanese minister to Korea, [Miura Gorō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miura_Gor%C5%8D "Miura Gorō"), orchestrated a plot against 43-year-old Queen Min (later given the title of " [Empress Myeongseong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empress_Myeongseong "Empress Myeongseong")"), and on 8 October 1895, she was assassinated by Japanese agents.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Dong-a_2002-0)[\[31\]](#refnote-Dong-a_2002) The Korean military unit, [Hullyŏndae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hully%C5%8Fndae "Hullyŏndae"), participated in the assassination. With Korean aid, Japanese assassins were allowed to enter the palace Gyeongbokgung. In 2001, Russian reports on the assassination were found in the archives of the Foreign Ministry of the Russian Federation. The documents included the testimony of King Gojong, several witnesses of the assassination, and [Karl Ivanovich Weber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Ivanovich_Weber "Karl Ivanovich Weber")'s report to [Aleksey Lobanov-Rostovsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleksey_Lobanov-Rostovsky "Aleksey Lobanov-Rostovsky"), the Foreign Minister of Russia, by Park Jonghyo. Weber was the _[chargé d'affaires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charg%C3%A9_d'affaires "Chargé d'affaires")_ at the Russian legation in Seoul at that time.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Dong-a_2002-1)[\[31\]](#refnote-Dong-a_2002) According to a Russian eyewitness, Seredin-Sabatin, an employee of the king, a group of Japanese agents entered [Gyeongbokgung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gyeongbokgung "Gyeongbokgung"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-33-0)[\[32\]](#refnote-33) killed Queen Min, and desecrated her body in the north wing of the palace.

The Heungseon Daewongun returned to the royal palace the same day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Dong-a_2002-2)[\[31\]](#refnote-Dong-a_2002) On 11 February 1896, [Gojong and the crown prince fled for protection at the Russian legation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gojong's_internal_exile_to_the_Russian_legation "Gojong's internal exile to the Russian legation") in Seoul, from which he governed for about a year.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:04-0)[\[33\]](#refnote-:04)

### Democracy protests and the proclamation of the Korean Empire

Main articles: [Independence Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Club "Independence Club") and [Korean Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Empire "Korean Empire")

In 1896, various Korean activists formed the [Independence Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Club "Independence Club"). They advocated a number of societal reforms, including democracy and a constitutional monarchy, and pushed for closer ties to Western countries to counterbalance Japanese influence. It went on to be influential in Korean politics for the short time that it operated, to the chagrin of Gojong. Gojong eventually forcefully disbanded the organization in 1898.[&#8202;](#ref-back-35-0)[\[34\]](#refnote-35)

In October 1897, Gojong returned to the palace [Deoksugung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deoksugung "Deoksugung"), and proclaimed the founding of the [Korean Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Empire "Korean Empire") at the royal altar [Hwangudan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hwangudan "Hwangudan"). This symbolicly asserted Korea's independence from China, especially as Gojong demolished a reception hall that was once used to entertain Chinese ambassadors to build the altar.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:05-0)[\[35\]](#refnote-:05)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:9-0)[\[36\]](#refnote-:9)

### Prelude to annexation

Main articles: [Russo-Japanese War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Japanese_War "Russo-Japanese War"), [Taft–Katsura agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taft%E2%80%93Katsura_agreement "Taft–Katsura agreement"), [Japan–Korea Treaty of 1905](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan%E2%80%93Korea_Treaty_of_1905 "Japan–Korea Treaty of 1905"), and [Hague Secret Emissary Affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hague_Secret_Emissary_Affair "Hague Secret Emissary Affair")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9c/%E7%B5%B1%E7%9B%A3%E5%BA%9C%E3%81%AE%E6%97%97.svg/500px-%E7%B5%B1%E7%9B%A3%E5%BA%9C%E3%81%AE%E6%97%97.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:%E7%B5%B1%E7%9B%A3%E5%BA%9C%E3%81%AE%E6%97%97.svg) Flag of the Japanese Resident General of Korea (1905–1910)

Having established economic and military dominance in Korea in October 1904, Japan reported that it had developed 25 reforms which it intended to introduce into Korea by gradual degrees. Among these was the intended acceptance by the Korean Financial Department of a Japanese Superintendent, the replacement of Korean Foreign Ministers and consuls by Japanese and the "union of military arms" in which the military of Korea would be modeled after the Japanese military.[&#8202;](#ref-back-38-0)[\[37\]](#refnote-38) These reforms were forestalled by the prosecution of the [Russo-Japanese War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Japanese_War "Russo-Japanese War") from 8 February 1904, to 5 September 1905, which Japan won, thus eliminating Japan's last rival to influence in Korea.[&#8202;](#ref-back-chosondy-0)[\[38\]](#refnote-chosondy)

Frustrated by this, King Gojong invited [Alice Roosevelt Longworth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alice_Roosevelt_Longworth "Alice Roosevelt Longworth"), who was on a tour of Asian countries with [William Howard Taft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Howard_Taft "William Howard Taft"), to the Imperial Palace on 20 September 1905, to seek political support from the United States despite her diplomatic rudeness. However, it was after exchanging opinions through the [Taft–Katsura agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taft%E2%80%93Katsura_agreement "Taft–Katsura agreement") on 27 July 1905, that America and Japan would not interfere with each other on colonial issues.[&#8202;](#ref-back-40-0)[\[39\]](#refnote-40)[&#8202;](#ref-back-41-0)[\[40\]](#refnote-41)

Under the [Treaty of Portsmouth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Portsmouth "Treaty of Portsmouth"), signed in September 1905, Russia acknowledged Japan's "paramount political, military, and economic interest" in Korea.[&#8202;](#ref-back-chosondy-1)[\[38\]](#refnote-chosondy)

Two months later, Korea was obliged to become a Japanese protectorate by the [Japan–Korea Treaty of 1905](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan%E2%80%93Korea_Treaty_of_1905 "Japan–Korea Treaty of 1905") and the "reforms" were enacted, including the reduction of the Korean Army from 20,000 to 1,000 men by disbanding all garrisons in the provinces, retaining only a single garrison in the precincts of Seoul.[&#8202;](#ref-back-chosondy-2)[\[38\]](#refnote-chosondy) On 6 January 1905, Horace Allen, head of the American Legation in Seoul reported to his Secretary of State, John Hay, that the Korean government had been advised by the Japanese government "that hereafter the police matters of Seoul will be controlled by the Japanese gendarmerie" and "that a Japanese police inspector will be placed in each prefecture".[&#8202;](#ref-back-42-0)[\[41\]](#refnote-42) A large number of Koreans organized themselves in education and reform movements, but Japanese dominance in Korea had become a reality.[&#8202;](#ref-back-chosondy-3)[\[38\]](#refnote-chosondy)

In June 1907, the [Second Peace Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hague_Conventions_of_1899_and_1907 "Hague Conventions of 1899 and 1907") was held in [The Hague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hague "The Hague"). Emperor Gojong secretly sent three representatives to bring the problems of Korea to the world's attention. The three envoys, who questioned the legality of the protectorate convention, were [refused access](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hague_Secret_Emissary_Affair "Hague Secret Emissary Affair") to the public debates by the international delegates. One of these representatives was missionary and historian [Homer Hulbert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homer_Hulbert "Homer Hulbert"). Out of despair, one of the Korean representatives, [Yi Tjoune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yi_Tjoune "Yi Tjoune"), committed suicide at The Hague.[&#8202;](#ref-back-43-0)[\[42\]](#refnote-43)

In response, the Japanese government took stronger measures. On 19 July 1907, Emperor Gojong was forced to relinquish his imperial authority and appoint the Crown Prince as regent. Japanese officials used this concession to force the accession of the new Emperor Sunjong following abdication, which was never agreed to by Gojong. Neither Gojong nor Sunjong were present at the 'accession' ceremony. Sunjong was to be the last ruler of the Joseon dynasty, founded in 1392.[&#8202;](#ref-back-44-0)[\[43\]](#refnote-44)

On 24 July 1907, a treaty was signed under the leadership of [Lee Wan-yong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Wan-yong "Lee Wan-yong") and former [Japanese Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_Prime_Minister "Japanese Prime Minister") [Ito Hirobumi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/It%C5%8D_Hirobumi "Itō Hirobumi") to transfer all rights of [Korea to Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan%E2%80%93Korea_Treaty_of_1907 "Japan–Korea Treaty of 1907"). This led to a large-scale righteous army movement among Koreans, and disbanded troops joined the resistance forces. Japan's response to this was a scorched earth tactic using division-sized troops, which resulted in the movement of armed resistance organizations in Korea to Manchuria and Imperial Russia. Amid this confusion, on 26 October 1909, [Ahn Jung-geun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahn_Jung-geun "Ahn Jung-geun"), a former volunteer soldier and a Korean nationalist, assassinated Hirobumi in [Harbin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harbin "Harbin").[&#8202;](#ref-back-45-0)[\[44\]](#refnote-45)

Meanwhile, pro-Japanese populist groups such as the [Iljinhoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iljinhoe "Iljinhoe") assisted Japan as they were fascinated by Japan's [pan-Asianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Asianism "Pan-Asianism"), hoping that Korea would have self-autonomy. They served as representative consultant for [Ryohei Uchida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ry%C5%8Dhei_Uchida "Ryōhei Uchida"), and were used for propaganda with the support of the Japanese government. On 3 December 1909, he and [Lee Wan-yong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Wan-yong "Lee Wan-yong") issued a statement demanding a political alliance with Japan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-46-0)[\[45\]](#refnote-46)

However, it took the form of Japan's annexation of Korean territory and these groups was disbanded by [Terauchi Masatake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terauchi_Masatake "Terauchi Masatake") on 26 September 1910.[&#8202;](#ref-back-47-0)[\[46\]](#refnote-47)[&#8202;](#ref-back-48-0)[\[47\]](#refnote-48)

### Militant resistance

Main article: [Righteous armies §During the Japanese colonial period (1910–1945)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Righteous_armies#During_the_Japanese_colonial_period_(1910%E2%80%931945) "Righteous armies")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/Les_Troubles_de_Coree.jpg/500px-Les_Troubles_de_Coree.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Les_Troubles_de_Coree.jpg) Battle of Namdaemun in 1907 – _[Le Petit Journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Petit_Journal_(newspaper) "Le Petit Journal (newspaper)")_

During the prelude to the 1910 annexation, a number of irregular civilian militias known as " [righteous armies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Righteous_armies "Righteous armies")" arose. They consisted of tens of thousands of peasants engaged in anti-Japanese armed rebellion. After the Korean army was disbanded in 1907, former soldiers joined the armies and fought the Japanese army at [Namdaemun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namdaemun "Namdaemun"). They were defeated, and largely fled into Manchuria, where they joined the guerrilla resistance movement that persisted until Korea's 1945 liberation.

### Military police

As Korean resistance against Japanese rule intensified, Japanese replaced the Korean police system with their military police, the _[Kempeitai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kempeitai "Kempeitai")_. [Akashi Motojiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akashi_Motojiro "Akashi Motojiro") was appointed the commander of Japanese military police forces. The Japanese eventually replaced Imperial Korean police forces in June 1910, and they combined police forces and military police, firmly establishing the rule of military police. After the annexation, Akashi started to serve as the Chief of Police. These military police officers started to have great authority over Koreans. Not only Japanese but also Koreans served as police officers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-49-0)[\[48\]](#refnote-49)

## Japan–Korea annexation treaty (1910)

Main article: [Japan–Korea Treaty of 1910](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan%E2%80%93Korea_Treaty_of_1910 "Japan–Korea Treaty of 1910")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/General_power_of_attorney_to_Lee_Wan-Yong_signed_and_sealed_by_Sunjong.jpg/500px-General_power_of_attorney_to_Lee_Wan-Yong_signed_and_sealed_by_Sunjong.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:General_power_of_attorney_to_Lee_Wan-Yong_signed_and_sealed_by_Sunjong.jpg) General power of attorney to [Lee Wan-yong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Wan-yong "Lee Wan-yong") sealed and signed, by the last emperor, [Sunjong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunjong_of_Korea "Sunjong of Korea") on 22 August 1910 (융희4년;隆熙4年)

In May 1910, the [Minister of War of Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_War_of_Japan "Ministry of War of Japan"), [Terauchi Masatake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terauchi_Masatake "Terauchi Masatake"), was given a mission to finalize Japanese control over Korea after the previous treaties (the [Japan–Korea Treaty of February 1904](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan%E2%80%93Korea_Treaty_of_February_1904 "Japan–Korea Treaty of February 1904") and the [Japan–Korea Treaty of 1907](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan%E2%80%93Korea_Treaty_of_1907 "Japan–Korea Treaty of 1907")) had made Korea a protectorate of Japan and had established Japanese hegemony over Korean domestic politics. On 22 August 1910, Japan effectively [annexed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annexation "Annexation") Korea with the [Japan–Korea Treaty of 1910](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan%E2%80%93Korea_Treaty_of_1910 "Japan–Korea Treaty of 1910") signed by [Ye Wanyong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ye_Wanyong "Ye Wanyong"), Prime Minister of Korea, and Terauchi Masatake, who became the first [Governor-General of Chōsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_Ch%C5%8Dsen "Governor-General of Chōsen").

The treaty became effective the same day and was published one week later. The treaty stipulated:

- Article 1: His Majesty the Emperor of Korea concedes completely and definitely his entire sovereignty over the whole Korean territory to His Majesty the Emperor of Japan.
- Article 2: His Majesty the Emperor of Japan accepts the concession stated in the previous article and consents to the annexation of Korea to the Empire of Japan.

Both the protectorate and the annexation treaties were declared already void in the 1965 [Treaty on Basic Relations between Japan and the Republic of Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_on_Basic_Relations_between_Japan_and_the_Republic_of_Korea "Treaty on Basic Relations between Japan and the Republic of Korea").

This period is also known as Military Police Reign Era (1910–19) in which police had the authority to rule the entire country. Japan was in control of the media, law, as well as government by physical power and regulations.

In March 2010, 109 Korean intellectuals and 105 Japanese intellectuals met on the 100th anniversary of [Japan–Korea Treaty of 1910](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan%E2%80%93Korea_Treaty_of_1910 "Japan–Korea Treaty of 1910") where they jointly declared this annexation treaty null and void. They declared these statements separately in their capital cities (Seoul and Tōkyō) with a simultaneous press conference. They announced the "Japanese empire pressured the outcry of the Korean Empire and people and forced by Japan–Korea Treaty of 1910 and full text of a treaty was false and text of the agreement was also false". They also declared the "Process and formality of "Japan–Korea Treaty of 1910" had huge deficiencies and therefore the treaty was null and void. This implied the [March First Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_First_Movement "March First Movement") was not an illegal movement.[&#8202;](#ref-back-50-0)[\[49\]](#refnote-50)[&#8202;](#ref-back-51-0)[\[50\]](#refnote-51)[&#8202;](#ref-back-52-0)[\[51\]](#refnote-52)

## Early years and expansion (1910–1941)

### Japanese migration and land ownership

From around the time of the First Sino-Japanese War of 1894–1895, Japanese merchants started settling in towns and cities in Korea seeking economic opportunity. By 1908 the number of Japanese settlers in Korea was somewhere below the figure of 500,000,[&#8202;](#ref-back-53-0)[\[52\]](#refnote-53) comprising one of the _[nikkei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_diaspora "Japanese diaspora")_ communities in the world at the time.

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Masatake_Terauchi_2.jpg/330px-Masatake_Terauchi_2.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Masatake_Terauchi_2.jpg) [Terauchi Masatake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terauchi_Masatake "Terauchi Masatake"), the first Governor-General[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/7/7f/Oriental_Development_Company.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Oriental_Development_Company.JPG) Headquarters of the [Oriental Development Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Development_Company "Oriental Development Company") in Keijō

Many Japanese settlers showed interest in acquiring agricultural land in Korea even before Japanese land-ownership was officially legalized in 1906. Governor-General Terauchi Masatake facilitated settlement through [land reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_reform "Land reform"). The Korean land-ownership system featured absentee landlords, only partial owner-tenants and cultivators with traditional (but no legal proof of) ownership. By 1920, 90 percent of Korean land had proper ownership of Koreans.[&#8202;](#ref-back-54-0)[\[53\]](#refnote-54) Terauchi's new Land Survey Bureau conducted [cadastral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadastral "Cadastral") surveys that established ownership on the basis of written proof (deeds, titles, and similar documents). The system denied ownership to those who could not provide such written documentation; these turned out to be mostly high-class and impartial owners who had only traditional verbal cultivator-rights.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\] Japanese landlords included both individuals and corporations (such as the [Oriental Development Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Development_Company "Oriental Development Company")). Because of these developments, Japanese landownership soared, as did the amount of land taken over by private Japanese companies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-55-0)[\[54\]](#refnote-55)

Many former Korean landowners, as well as agricultural workers, became [tenant farmers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenant_farmer "Tenant farmer"), having lost their [entitlements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Property "Property") almost overnight because they could not pay for the land reclamation and irrigation improvements forced on them.[&#8202;](#ref-back-56-0)[\[55\]](#refnote-56) Compounding the economic stresses imposed on the Korean peasantry, the authorities forced Korean peasants to do long days of compulsory labor to build irrigation works; Japanese imperial officials made peasants pay for these projects in the form of heavy taxes, impoverishing many of them and causing even more of them lose their land.[&#8202;](#ref-back-57-0)[\[56\]](#refnote-57) Although many other subsequent developments placed ever greater strain on Korea's peasants, Japan's rice shortage in 1918 was the greatest catalyst for hardship. During that shortage, Japan looked to Korea for increased rice cultivation; as Korean peasants started producing more for Japan, however, the amount they took to eat dropped precipitously, causing much resentment among them.[&#8202;](#ref-back-58-0)[\[57\]](#refnote-58)

By 1910 an estimated 7 to 8% of all [arable land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arable_land "Arable land") in Korea had come under Japanese control. This ratio increased steadily; as of the years 1916, 1920, and 1932, the ratio of Japanese land ownership increased from 36.8 to 39.8 to 52.7%. The level of tenancy was similar to that of farmers in Japan itself; however, in Korea, the landowners were mostly Japanese, while the tenants were all Koreans. As often occurred in Japan itself, tenants had to pay over half their crop in rent.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Japan_Focus-0)[\[58\]](#refnote-Japan_Focus)

By the 1930s the growth of the urban economy and the exodus of farmers to the cities had gradually weakened the hold of the landlords. With the growth of the wartime economy throughout the [Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World_War "Second World War"), the government recognized [landlordism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landlordism "Landlordism") as an impediment to increased agricultural productivity, and took steps to increase control over the rural sector through the formation in Japan in 1943 of the Central Agricultural Association (中央農会, _chūō nōkai_), a compulsory organization under the wartime [command economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Command_economy "Command economy").\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

The Japanese government had hoped emigration to its colonies would mitigate the population boom in the _[naichi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naichi "Naichi")_(内地),[&#8202;](#ref-back-kimura-0)[\[59\]](#refnote-kimura) but had largely failed to accomplish this by 1936.[&#8202;](#ref-back-porter-0)[\[60\]](#refnote-porter) According to figures from 1934, Japanese in Chōsen numbered approximately 561,000 out of a total population of over 21 million, less than 3%. By 1939 the Japanese population increased to 651,000, mostly from Japan's western prefectures. During the same period, the population in Chōsen grew faster than that in the _naichi_. Koreans also migrated to the _naichi_ in large numbers, especially after 1930; by 1939 there were over 981,000 Koreans living in Japan. Challenges which deterred Japanese from migrating into Chōsen included lack of arable land and population density comparable to that of Japan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-porter-1)[\[60\]](#refnote-porter)[&#8202;](#ref-back-kimura-1)[\[59\]](#refnote-kimura)

### Anthropology and cultural heritage

See also: [Korean History Compilation Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_History_Compilation_Committee "Korean History Compilation Committee")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/34/Hyochangwon_as_Korea%27s_first_golf_course.jpg/500px-Hyochangwon_as_Korea%27s_first_golf_course.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Hyochangwon_as_Korea's_first_golf_course.jpg) In 1921, Japan turned a Korean royal cemetery into a golf course, with the graves still directly on the course. This occurred at what is now [Hyochang Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyochang_Park "Hyochang Park").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:7-0)[\[61\]](#refnote-:7)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:5-0)[\[62\]](#refnote-:5)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:4-0)[\[63\]](#refnote-:4)

Japan sent anthropologists to Korea who took photos of the traditional state of Korean villages, serving as evidence that Korea was "backwards" and needed to be modernized.[&#8202;](#ref-back-65-0)[\[64\]](#refnote-65)

In 1925, the Japanese government established the [Korean History Compilation Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_History_Compilation_Committee "Korean History Compilation Committee"), and it was administered by the Governor-General and engaged in collecting Korean historical materials and compiling Korean history.[&#8202;](#ref-back-66-0)[\[65\]](#refnote-66) According to the _[Doosan Encyclopedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doosan_Encyclopedia "Doosan Encyclopedia")_, some mythology was incorporated.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Korean_History_Compilation_Committee-0)[\[66\]](#refnote-Korean_History_Compilation_Committee) The committee supported the theory of a Japanese colony on the Korean Peninsula called [Mimana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mimana "Mimana"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-Korean_History_Compilation_Committee-1)[\[66\]](#refnote-Korean_History_Compilation_Committee) which, according to E. Taylor Atkins, is "among the most disputed issues in East Asian historiography."[&#8202;](#ref-back-68-0)[\[67\]](#refnote-68)

Japan executed the first modern archaeological excavations in Korea.[&#8202;](#ref-back-69-0)[\[68\]](#refnote-69) The Japanese administration also relocated some artifacts; for instance, a stone monument (棕蟬縣神祠碑), which was originally located in the [Liaodong Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liaodong_Peninsula "Liaodong Peninsula"), then under [Japanese control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kwantung_Leased_Territory "Kwantung Leased Territory"), was taken out of its context and moved to [Pyongyang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyongyang "Pyongyang").[&#8202;](#ref-back-70-0)[\[69\]](#refnote-70) As of April 2020, 81,889 Korean cultural artifacts are in Japan. According to the Overseas Korean Cultural Heritage Foundation, not all the artifacts were moved illegally. Adding to the challenge of repatriating illegally exported Korean cultural properties is the lack of experts in Korean art at overseas museums and institutions, alterations made to artifacts that obscure their origin, and that moving Korean artifacts within what was previously internationally recognized Japanese territory was lawful at the time.[&#8202;](#ref-back-The_Korea_Times-0)[\[70\]](#refnote-The_Korea_Times)[&#8202;](#ref-back-72-0)[\[71\]](#refnote-72)[&#8202;](#ref-back-tussle-0)[\[72\]](#refnote-tussle)[&#8202;](#ref-back-74-0)[\[73\]](#refnote-74) The South Korean government has been continuing its efforts to repatriate Korean artifacts from museums and private collections overseas.[&#8202;](#ref-back-The_Korea_Times-1)[\[70\]](#refnote-The_Korea_Times)

The royal palace [Gyeongbokgung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gyeongbokgung "Gyeongbokgung") was partially destroyed beginning in the 1910s, to make way for the [Japanese General Government Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_General_Government_Building,_Seoul "Japanese General Government Building, Seoul") as well as the colonial [Chōsen Industrial Exhibition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ch%C5%8Dsen_Industrial_Exhibition "Chōsen Industrial Exhibition").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-0)[\[74\]](#refnote-:1) Hundreds of historic buildings in [Deoksugung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deoksugung "Deoksugung") were also destroyed to make way for the [Yi Royal Family Museum of Fine Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yi_Royal_Family_Museum_of_Fine_Art?action=edit&redlink=1 "Yi Royal Family Museum of Fine Art")\[ [ko](https://ko.wikipedia.org/wiki/%EC%9D%B4%EC%99%95%EA%B0%80%EB%AF%B8%EC%88%A0%EA%B4%80 "ko:이왕가미술관")\]. The displays in the museum reportedly intentionally contrasted traditional Korean art with examples of modern Japanese art, to portray Japan as progressive and legitimize Japanese rule.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-1)[\[74\]](#refnote-:1) The [National Palace Museum of Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Palace_Museum_of_Korea "National Palace Museum of Korea"), originally built as the Korean Imperial Museum in 1908 to preserve the treasures in the [Gyeongbokgung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gyeongbokgung "Gyeongbokgung"), was retained under the Japanese administration but renamed Museum of the Yi Dynasty in 1938.[&#8202;](#ref-back-76-0)[\[75\]](#refnote-76)

The Governor-General instituted a law in 1933 to preserve Korea's most important historical artifacts. The system established by this law, retained as the present-day [National Treasures of South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Treasures_of_South_Korea "National Treasures of South Korea") and [National Treasures of North Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Treasures_of_North_Korea "National Treasures of North Korea"), was intended to preserve Korean historical artifacts, including those not yet unearthed. Japan's 1871 Edict for the Preservation of Antiquities and Old Items could not be automatically applied to Korea due to Japanese law, which required an imperial ordinance to apply the edict in Korea. The 1933 law to protect Korean cultural heritages was based on the Japanese 1871 edict.[&#8202;](#ref-back-hamada.u-shimane.ac.jp-0)[\[76\]](#refnote-hamada.u-shimane.ac.jp)

### Anti-Chinese riots of 1931

Main article: [Wanpaoshan Incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wanpaoshan_Incident "Wanpaoshan Incident")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/Chinese_anti-Japanese_poster_published_after_the_revenge_by_Koreans.jpg/500px-Chinese_anti-Japanese_poster_published_after_the_revenge_by_Koreans.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Chinese_anti-Japanese_poster_published_after_the_revenge_by_Koreans.jpg) Chinese anti-Japanese poster published after reprisals by Koreans

Due to a waterway construction permit, in the small town of Wanpaoshan in Manchuria near [Changchun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Changchun "Changchun"), "violent clashes" broke out between the local Chinese and Korean immigrants on 2 July 1931. _[The Chosun Ilbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Chosun_Ilbo "The Chosun Ilbo")_, a major Korean newspaper, misreported that many Koreans had died in the clashes, sparking a Chinese exclusion movement in urban areas of the Korean Peninsula.[&#8202;](#ref-back-78-0)[\[77\]](#refnote-78) The worst of the rioting occurred in [Pyongyang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyongyang "Pyongyang") on 5 July. Approximately 127 Chinese people were killed, 393 wounded, and a considerable number of properties were destroyed by Korean residents.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Wanpaoshan-0)[\[78\]](#refnote-Wanpaoshan) [Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_China_(1912%E2%80%931949) "Republic of China (1912–1949)") further alleged the Japanese authorities in Korea did not take adequate steps to protect the lives and property of the Chinese residents, and blamed the authorities for allowing inflammatory accounts to be published. As a result of this riot, the Minister of Foreign Affairs [Kijūrō Shidehara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kij%C5%ABr%C5%8D_Shidehara "Kijūrō Shidehara"), who insisted on Japanese, Chinese, and Korean harmony, lost his position.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Wanpaoshan-1)[\[78\]](#refnote-Wanpaoshan)

### Order to change names

Main article: [Sōshi-kaimei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C5%8Dshi-kaimei "Sōshi-kaimei")

In 1911, the proclamation "Matter Concerning the Changing of Korean Names" ( _朝鮮人ノ姓名改称ニ関スル件_) was issued, barring ethnic Koreans from taking Japanese names and retroactively reverting the names of Koreans who had already registered under Japanese names back to the original Korean ones.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Mizuno1-0)[\[79\]](#refnote-Mizuno1) By 1939, however, this position was reversed and Japan's focus had shifted towards [cultural assimilation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_assimilation "Cultural assimilation") of the Korean people; Imperial Decree 19 and 20 on Korean Civil Affairs ( [Sōshi-kaimei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E5%89%B5%E6%B0%8F%E6%94%B9%E5%90%8D "創氏改名")) went into effect, whereby ethnic Koreans were forced to surrender their traditional use of clan-based [Korean family name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_name#Family_names "Korean name") system, in favor of a new surname to be used in the family register. The surname could be of their own choosing, including their native clan name, but in practice many Koreans received a Japanese surname. There is controversy over whether or not the adoption of a Japanese surname was effectively mandatory, or merely strongly encouraged.[&#8202;](#ref-back-81-0)[\[80\]](#refnote-81)[&#8202;](#ref-back-82-0)[\[81\]](#refnote-82)

## World War II

### National Mobilization Law

See also: [Koreans in Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koreans_in_Japan "Koreans in Japan") and [Sakhalin Koreans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sakhalin_Koreans "Sakhalin Koreans")

#### Forcing of labor and migration

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Japan-Korea_Cooperative_Unity_World_Leader_Postcard_1920s.png/330px-Japan-Korea_Cooperative_Unity_World_Leader_Postcard_1920s.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Japan-Korea_Cooperative_Unity_World_Leader_Postcard_1920s.png) _Japan-Korea Cooperative Unity, World Leader._ – The notion of racial and imperial unity of Korea and Japan gained widespread following among the literate minority of the middle and upper classes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-84-0)[\[83\]](#refnote-84)[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Kuniaki_Koiso_2.jpg/330px-Kuniaki_Koiso_2.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Kuniaki_Koiso_2.jpg) [Kuniaki Koiso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuniaki_Koiso "Kuniaki Koiso"), Governor-General of Chōsen from 1942 to 1944, implemented a draft of Koreans for wartime labor.

From 1939, [labor shortages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_shortage "Labour shortage") as a result of [conscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscription "Conscription") of Japanese men for the military efforts of World War II led to organized official recruitment of Koreans to work in mainland Japan, initially through civilian agents, and later directly, often involving elements of coercion. As the labor shortage increased, by 1942, the Japanese authorities extended the provisions of the [National Mobilization Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Mobilization_Law "National Mobilization Law") to include the conscription of Korean workers for factories and mines in Korea, [Manchukuo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchukuo "Manchukuo"), and the involuntary relocation of workers to Japan itself as needed.

The combination of immigrants and forced laborers during World War II brought the total to over 2 million Koreans in Japan by the end of the war, according to estimates by the [Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Commander_for_the_Allied_Powers "Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ryang2000-Ch2-0)[\[84\]](#refnote-Ryang2000-Ch2)

Of the 5,400,000 Koreans conscripted, about 670,000 were taken to mainland Japan (including [Karafuto Prefecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karafuto_Prefecture "Karafuto Prefecture"), present-day [Sakhalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sakhalin "Sakhalin"), now part of Russia) for civilian labor. Those who were brought to Japan were often forced to work under conditions that have been described as appalling and dangerous.[&#8202;](#ref-back-86-0)[\[85\]](#refnote-86) Although Koreans were reportedly treated better than laborers from other countries, large numbers still died. In Japan, 60,000 of the 670,000 mobilized laborers died. In Korea and Manchuria, estimates of deaths range between 270,000 and 810,000.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Rummel_1999-0)[\[86\]](#refnote-Rummel_1999)

Korean laborers were also found as far as the [Tarawa Atoll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarawa "Tarawa"), where during the [Battle of Tarawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Tarawa "Battle of Tarawa") only 129 of the 1200 laborers survived.[&#8202;](#ref-back-88-0)[\[87\]](#refnote-88) According to testimonies in Japanese records, Korean laborers on the [Mili Atoll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mili_Atoll "Mili Atoll") were given "whale meat" to consume, which was actually human flesh from other dead Koreans. They rebelled after learning the truth, and were killed by the dozens in the aftermath.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:32-0)[\[88\]](#refnote-:32)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:14-0)[\[89\]](#refnote-:14)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:23-0)[\[90\]](#refnote-:23) Korean laborers also worked in Korea itself, notably in [Jeju](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeju_Island "Jeju Island") where in the later stages of the [Pacific War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_War "Pacific War"), Korean laborers expanded airfields and built facilities at [Altteureu Airfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altteureu_Airfield "Altteureu Airfield") to block a US invasion of the Japanese mainland and in 1945 laborers on [Songaksan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Songaksan_(Jeju) "Songaksan (Jeju)") (where several airstrips were located) were ordered to smooth down the slope to prevent American tanks being able to go up.[&#8202;](#ref-back-92-0)[\[91\]](#refnote-92)

Most Korean atomic-bomb victims in Japan had been drafted for work at military industrial factories in Hiroshima and Nagasaki.[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBC_News-0)[\[92\]](#refnote-BBC_News) In the name of humanitarian assistance, Japan paid South Korea four billion yen (approx. thirty five million dollars) and built a welfare center for those suffering from the effects of the atomic bomb.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Andreas_Hippin-0)[\[93\]](#refnote-Andreas_Hippin)

#### Korean service in the Japanese military

Korean military participation until 1943[&#8202;](#ref-back-95-0)[\[94\]](#refnote-95)[&#8202;](#ref-back-96-0)[\[95\]](#refnote-96)[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Japan_%26_Korea_Imperial_family_Yasukuni_1938-10_%28cropped%29.jpg/500px-Japan_%26_Korea_Imperial_family_Yasukuni_1938-10_%28cropped%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Japan_&_Korea_Imperial_family_Yasukuni_1938-10_(cropped).jpg) Korean royalty of the [House of Yi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Yi "House of Yi") (front row, right to left): [Yi Wu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yi_Wu "Yi Wu"), [Yi Geon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yi_Geon "Yi Geon") and [Yi Un](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yi_Un "Yi Un") as officers of the Imperial Japanese Army, together with members of the Japanese imperial family at the [Yasukuni Shrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasukuni_Shrine "Yasukuni Shrine"), 1938

Japan did not draft ethnic Koreans into its military until 1944 when the tide of World War II turned against it. Until 1944, enlistment in the [Imperial Japanese Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Japanese_Army "Imperial Japanese Army") by ethnic Koreans was voluntary, and highly competitive. From a 14% acceptance rate in 1938, it dropped to a 2% acceptance rate in 1943 while the raw number of applicants increased from 3000 per annum to 300,000 in just five years during World War II.[&#8202;](#ref-back-98-0)[\[97\]](#refnote-98) Recent scholarly research indicates that the Japanese authorities employed substantial coercive and administrative pressure during the recruitment of Koreans into military service in the late colonial era. According to historian Pyo Young-Soo, while the authorities formally characterized the process as "voluntary enlistment," archival documentation reveals systemic use of coercive mechanisms, such as issuing enlistment orders and invoking regulatory authority to secure compliance.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Pyo2008-0)[\[98\]](#refnote-Pyo2008)

Motivation of Korean Applicants in 1941[&#8202;](#ref-back-100-0)[\[99\]](#refnote-100)[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/37/Park_Japan.JPG/330px-Park_Japan.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Park_Japan.JPG) [Park Chung Hee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Park_Chung_Hee "Park Chung Hee"), future leader of South Korea, as a soldier of the Manchukuo Imperial Army

Other Korean officers who served Japan moved on to successful careers in post-colonial South Korea. Examples include [Park Chung Hee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Park_Chung_Hee "Park Chung Hee"), who became president of South Korea; [Chung Il-kwon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chung_Il-kwon "Chung Il-kwon"), prime minister from 1964 to 1970; [Paik Sun-yup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paik_Sun-yup "Paik Sun-yup"), South Korea's youngest general who was famous for his command of the [1st Infantry Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1st_Infantry_Division_(South_Korea) "1st Infantry Division (South Korea)") during the [defense of the Pusan Perimeter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Pusan_Perimeter "Battle of Pusan Perimeter"), and [Kim Suk-won](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Suk-won_(general) "Kim Suk-won (general)"), a colonel of the Imperial Japanese Army who subsequently became a general of the South Korean army. The first ten of the Chiefs of Army Staff of South Korea graduated from the [Imperial Japanese Army Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Japanese_Army_Academy "Imperial Japanese Army Academy") and none from the [Korean Liberation Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Liberation_Army "Korean Liberation Army").[&#8202;](#ref-back-101-0)[\[100\]](#refnote-101)[&#8202;](#ref-back-102-0)[\[101\]](#refnote-102)

Officer cadets had been joining the Japanese Army since before the annexation by attending the Imperial Japanese Army Academy. Enlisted soldier recruitment began as early as 1938, when the Japanese [Kwantung Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kwantung_Army "Kwantung Army") in Manchuria began accepting pro-Japanese Korean volunteers into the army of Manchukuo, and formed the [Gando Special Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gando_Special_Force "Gando Special Force"). Koreans in this unit specialized in counter-insurgency operations against communist guerillas in the region of [Jiandao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiandao "Jiandao"). The size of the unit grew considerably at an annual rate of 700 men, and included such notable Koreans as General [Paik Sun-yup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paik_Sun-yup "Paik Sun-yup"), who served in the Korean War. Historian Philip Jowett noted that during the Japanese occupation of Manchuria, the Gando Special Force "earned a reputation for brutality and was reported to have laid waste to large areas which came under its rule".[&#8202;](#ref-back-103-0)[\[102\]](#refnote-103)

Starting in 1944, Japan started the [conscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscription "Conscription") of Koreans into the armed forces. All Korean men were drafted to either join the Imperial Japanese Army, as of April 1944, or work in the military industrial sector, as of September 1944. Before 1944, 18,000 Koreans passed the examination for induction into the army. Koreans provided workers to mines and construction sites around Japan. The number of conscripted Koreans reached its peak in 1944 in preparation for war.[&#8202;](#ref-back-104-0)[\[103\]](#refnote-104) From 1944, about 200,000 Korean men were inducted into the army.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

During World War II, American soldiers frequently encountered Korean soldiers within the ranks of the Imperial Japanese Army. Most notably was in the [Battle of Tarawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Tarawa "Battle of Tarawa"), which was considered during that time to be one of the bloodiest battles in U.S. military history. A fifth of the Japanese garrison during this battle consisted of Korean laborers, where on the last night of the battle a combined 300 Japanese soldiers and Korean laborers did a last ditch charge. Like their Japanese counterparts, many of them were killed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-105-0)[\[104\]](#refnote-105)[&#8202;](#ref-back-106-0)[\[105\]](#refnote-106)

The Japanese, however, did not always believe they could rely on Korean laborers to fight alongside them. In _Prisoners of the Japanese_, author Gaven Daws wrote, "\[O\]n [Tinian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tinian "Tinian") there were five thousand Korean laborers and so as not to have hostiles _at their back_ when the Americans invaded, the Japanese killed them."[&#8202;](#ref-back-107-0)[\[106\]](#refnote-107)

After the war, 148 Koreans were convicted of Class B and C [Japanese war crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_war_crimes "Japanese war crimes"), 23 of whom were sentenced to death (compared to 920 Japanese who were sentenced to death), including Korean prison guards who were particularly notorious for their brutality during the war. The figure is relatively high considering that ethnic Koreans made up a small percentage of the Japanese military. Judge [Bert Röling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bert_R%C3%B6ling "Bert Röling"), who represented the Netherlands at the [International Military Tribunal for the Far East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Military_Tribunal_for_the_Far_East "International Military Tribunal for the Far East"), noted that "many of the commanders and guards in POW camps were Koreans – the Japanese apparently did not trust them as soldiers – and it is said that they were sometimes far more cruel than the Japanese."[&#8202;](#ref-back-108-0)[\[107\]](#refnote-108) In his memoirs, Colonel Eugene C. Jacobs wrote that during the [Bataan Death March](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bataan_Death_March "Bataan Death March"), "the Korean guards were the most abusive. The Japanese didn't trust them in battle, so used them as service troops; the Koreans were anxious to get blood on their bayonets; and then they thought they were veterans."[&#8202;](#ref-back-109-0)[\[108\]](#refnote-109)[&#8202;](#ref-back-110-0)[\[109\]](#refnote-110)

Korean guards were sent to the remote jungles of [Burma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burma "Burma"), where Lt. Col. William A. (Bill) Henderson wrote from his own experience that some of the guards overseeing the construction of the [Burma Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burma_Railway "Burma Railway") "were moronic and at times almost bestial in their treatment of prisoners. This applied particularly to Korean private soldiers, conscripted only for guard and sentry duties in many parts of the Japanese empire. Regrettably, they were appointed as guards for the prisoners throughout the camps of Burma and Siam."[&#8202;](#ref-back-111-0)[\[110\]](#refnote-111) The highest-ranking Korean to be prosecuted after the war was Lieutenant General [Hong Sa-ik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Sa-ik "Hong Sa-ik"), who was in command of all the Japanese prisoner-of-war camps in the Philippines.

### Comfort women

Main article: [Comfort women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comfort_women "Comfort women")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Korean_Comfort_Women_recorded_by_U.S._Marine_Corps.png/500px-Korean_Comfort_Women_recorded_by_U.S._Marine_Corps.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Korean_Comfort_Women_recorded_by_U.S._Marine_Corps.png) Korean comfort women on Okinawa being interviewed by U.S. marines after liberation

During [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II "World War II") many ethnic Korean girls and women (mostly aged 12–17) were forced by the Japanese military to become sex slaves on the pretext of being hired for jobs such as a seamstresses or factory workers and were forced to provide sexual service for Japanese soldiers by agencies or their families against their wishes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-112-0)[\[111\]](#refnote-112)[&#8202;](#ref-back-113-0)[\[112\]](#refnote-113)[&#8202;](#ref-back-114-0)[\[113\]](#refnote-114)[&#8202;](#ref-back-115-0)[\[114\]](#refnote-115) These women were euphemistically called " [comfort women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comfort_women "Comfort women")".

According to an interrogation report by [U.S. Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Army "U.S. Army") in 1944, comfort women were in good physical health. They were able to have a checkup once a week and to receive treatment lest they spread disease to the Japanese soldiers but not for their own health.[&#8202;](#ref-back-116-0)[\[115\]](#refnote-116) However a 1996 United Nations Report detailed that "large numbers of women were forced to submit to prolonged prostitution under conditions which were frequently indescribably traumatic". Documents that survived the war revealed "beyond doubt the extent to which the Japanese forces took direct responsibility for the comfort stations" and that the published practices were "in stark contrast with the brutality and cruelty of the practice".[&#8202;](#ref-back-117-0)[\[116\]](#refnote-117) [Chizuko Ueno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chizuko_Ueno "Chizuko Ueno") at [Kyoto University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyoto_University "Kyoto University") cautions against the claim that women were not forced as the fact that "no positive sources exist supporting claims that comfort women were forced labor" must be treated with doubt, since "it is well known that the great majority of potentially damaging official documents were destroyed in anticipation of the Allied occupation".[&#8202;](#ref-back-118-0)[\[117\]](#refnote-118)

The [Asian Women's Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Women's_Fund "Asian Women's Fund") claimed that during World War II the Imperial Japanese Army recruited anywhere from tens of thousands to hundreds of thousands of women from occupied territories to be used as sex slaves.[&#8202;](#ref-back-119-0)[\[118\]](#refnote-119) [Yoshimi Yoshiaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoshimi_Yoshiaki "Yoshimi Yoshiaki") asserted that possibly hundreds of thousands of girls and women, mainly from China and the Korean Peninsula but also Southeast Asian countries occupied by the Imperial Japanese Army, as well as Australia and the Netherlands, were forced to serve as comfort women. According to testimonies, young women were abducted from their homes in countries under Imperial Japanese rule. In many cases women were lured with promises of work in factories or restaurants. In some cases propaganda advocated equity and the sponsorship of women in higher education. Other enticements were false advertising for nursing jobs at outposts or Japanese army bases; once recruited, they were incarcerated in comfort stations both inside their nations and abroad.[&#8202;](#ref-back-120-0)[\[119\]](#refnote-120)[&#8202;](#ref-back-121-0)[\[120\]](#refnote-121)

From the early nineties onward, former Korean comfort women have continued to protest against the Japanese government for apparent historical negationism of crimes committed by the [Imperial Japanese Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Japanese_Army "Imperial Japanese Army") and have sought compensation for their sufferings during the war.[&#8202;](#ref-back-122-0)[\[121\]](#refnote-122)[&#8202;](#ref-back-123-0)[\[122\]](#refnote-123)[&#8202;](#ref-back-124-0)[\[123\]](#refnote-124) There has also been international support for compensation, such as from the European Union,[&#8202;](#ref-back-125-0)[\[124\]](#refnote-125) the Netherlands, Canada and the Philippines.[&#8202;](#ref-back-126-0)[\[125\]](#refnote-126) The United States passed [House of Representatives House Resolution 121](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives_House_Resolution_121 "United States House of Representatives House Resolution 121") on 30 July 2007, asking the Japanese government to redress the situation and to incorporate comfort women into school curriculum.[&#8202;](#ref-back-127-0)[\[126\]](#refnote-127) [Hirofumi Hayashi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hirofumi_Hayashi "Hirofumi Hayashi") at the [University of Manchester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Manchester "University of Manchester") argues that the resolution has helped to counter the "arguments of ultrarightists flooding the mainstream mass media" and warned against the rationalization of the comfort women system.[&#8202;](#ref-back-128-0)[\[127\]](#refnote-128)

### Religion and ideology

Protestant Christian missionary efforts in Asia were quite successful in Korea. American Presbyterians and Methodists arrived in the 1880s and were well received. They served as medical and educational missionaries, establishing schools and hospitals in numerous cities. In the years when Korea was under Japanese control, some Koreans adopted Christianity as an expression of nationalism in opposition to the Japan's efforts to promote the Japanese language and the Shinto religion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Danielle_Kane_2009_pp_365-0)[\[128\]](#refnote-Danielle_Kane_2009_pp_365)
In 1914 of 16 million Koreans, there were 86,000 Protestants and 79,000 Catholics. By 1934 the numbers were 168,000 and 147,000, respectively. Presbyterian missionaries were especially successful. Harmonizing with traditional practices became an issue. The Protestants developed a substitute for Confucian ancestral rites by merging Confucian-based and Christian death and funerary rituals.[&#8202;](#ref-back-130-0)[\[129\]](#refnote-130)

## Korean independence movement

Main article: [Korean independence movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_independence_movement "Korean independence movement")

### Guerrilla resistance in Manchuria and Russia

Since the early 1900s, numerous Koreans based in [Manchuria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchuria "Manchuria") and [Primorsky Krai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primorsky_Krai "Primorsky Krai") in Russia waged a guerrilla war against the Japanese occupation. [Beom-do Hong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Beom-do "Hong Beom-do")'s unit ambushed and annihilated the Imperial Japanese Army that was advancing in [Battle of Bongodong (Fengwudong)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Fengwudong "Battle of Fengwudong") in June 1920. The combined forces of the independence army commanded by [Kim Chwajin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Chwajin "Kim Chwajin") and Hong, while repeatedly retreating operationally, ambushed and killed about 1,500 Imperial Japanese soldiers in [Battle of Cheongsanri (Qīngshānlǐ)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Qingshanli "Battle of Qingshanli"). In retaliation to the heavy losses at the Battle of Cheongsanri, the [Imperial Japanese Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Japanese_Army "Imperial Japanese Army") committed the [Gando massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gando_massacre "Gando massacre"), massacring between 5,000 and tens of thousands of Korean civilians in [Gando](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiandao "Jiandao"). The [Japanese invasion of Manchuria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_invasion_of_Manchuria "Japanese invasion of Manchuria") in 1932 and subsequent [Pacification of Manchukuo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacification_of_Manchukuo "Pacification of Manchukuo") deprived many of these groups of their bases of operation and supplies. Many were forced to either flee elsewhere in China, or to join the [Red Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Army "Red Army")-backed forces in eastern Russia. One of the guerrilla leaders in this region was the future dictator of [North Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Korea "North Korea"), [Kim Il Sung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Il_Sung "Kim Il Sung"). These experiences served as a basis for Kim's legitimacy after the liberation of Korea.[&#8202;](#ref-back-131-0)[\[130\]](#refnote-131)

### March First Movement

Main article: [March First Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_First_Movement "March First Movement")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/%28Red_Cross_pamphlet_on_March_1st_Movement%29_%28KADA-shyun15-012~20%29_%28cropped%29.jpg/500px-%28Red_Cross_pamphlet_on_March_1st_Movement%29_%28KADA-shyun15-012~20%29_%28cropped%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:(Red_Cross_pamphlet_on_March_1st_Movement)_(KADA-shyun15-012~20)_(cropped).jpg) Korean Christians were [crucified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucifixion "Crucifixion") in the aftermath of the 1 March Movement protests (1919)

In January 1919, Emperor Gojong died suddenly, which led to widespread [theories that he had been poisoned by Japanese agents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gojong_poisoning_conspiracy_theories?action=edit&redlink=1 "Gojong poisoning conspiracy theories")\[ [ko](https://ko.wikipedia.org/wiki/%EA%B3%A0%EC%A2%85%20%EB%8F%85%EC%82%B4%EC%84%A4 "ko:고종 독살설")\]. Anti-Japanese sentiment flared amongst Koreans. In Tokyo, Korean students issued a [February 8 Declaration of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/February_8_Declaration_of_Independence "February 8 Declaration of Independence") that declared Korea independent from Japan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:24-0)[\[131\]](#refnote-:24) Inspired by this, Koreans in Seoul issued their own [declaration of independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Declaration_of_Independence "Korean Declaration of Independence"), which was prominently read aloud in [Tapgol Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tapgol_Park "Tapgol Park").[&#8202;](#ref-back-133-0)[\[132\]](#refnote-133) This gave rise to the nationwide 1 March Movement peaceful protests; it is estimated that 2million people took part in these rallies. However, they were violently suppressed by Japan; according to Korean records, over a year of demonstrations, 46,948 were arrested, 7,509 killed and 15,961 wounded.[&#8202;](#ref-back-134-0)[\[133\]](#refnote-134)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-0)[\[134\]](#refnote-:0) According to Japanese figures, 8,437 were arrested, 553 killed and 1,409 wounded.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-1)[\[134\]](#refnote-:0)

### Provisional Government of the Republic of Korea

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/42/Provisional_Government_of_the_Republic_of_Korea.jpg/500px-Provisional_Government_of_the_Republic_of_Korea.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Provisional_Government_of_the_Republic_of_Korea.jpg) Early members of the Provisional Government of the Republic of Korea (1919)

Main article: [Provisional Government of the Republic of Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisional_Government_of_the_Republic_of_Korea "Provisional Government of the Republic of Korea")

After the repression of the 1 March Movement protests, Koreans fled the peninsula.
