A number of them congregated in Shanghai a month after the protests and founded a government-in-exile: the Korean Provisional Government (KPG).[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERobinson200752–53-0)[\[135\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERobinson200752–53) The government was highly diverse, with both left- and right-leaning members, and engaged in a range of political and militant efforts that advocated for Korea's independence.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:42-0)[\[136\]](#refnote-:42) In 1931, member [Kim Ku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Ku "Kim Ku") founded the [Korean Patriotic Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Patriotic_Organization "Korean Patriotic Organization") (KPO), a militant arm of the KPG. The KPO planned a number of attacks on Japanese government and colonial officials, including a [1932 assassination attempt on Emperor Hirohito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sakuradamon_incident_(1932) "Sakuradamon incident (1932)") and a bombing at a [military rally in Shanghai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hongkou_Park_Incident "Hongkou Park Incident").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-0)[\[137\]](#refnote-:3)

After the Shanghai bombing, they were forced to flee the city, and eventually settled in Chongqing,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Encyclopedia_KLA-0)[\[138\]](#refnote-Encyclopedia_KLA)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-1)[\[137\]](#refnote-:3) where they received support from the [Chinese government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_China_(1912%E2%80%931949) "Republic of China (1912–1949)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-2)[\[137\]](#refnote-:3) There, Kim Ku, then the leader of the KPG, founded the [Korean Liberation Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Liberation_Army "Korean Liberation Army") (KLA). The army fought in China and Burma, and prepared for its return to Korea as the tide of World War II turned against Japan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Encyclopedia_KLA-1)[\[138\]](#refnote-Encyclopedia_KLA) This culminated in the [Eagle Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eagle_Project "Eagle Project"), a mission for the KPG and KLA to return to the peninsula and fight the Japanese. Ultimately, with the surprise of the [atomic bomb droppings on Nagasaki and Hiroshima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_bombings_of_Hiroshima_and_Nagasaki "Atomic bombings of Hiroshima and Nagasaki") and the sudden end of the war, the mission did not come to pass.[&#8202;](#ref-back-140-0)[\[139\]](#refnote-140)

### Resistance within Korea

Within Korea itself, anti-Japanese rallies continued on multiple occasions. In 1926, the [June Tenth Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/June_Tenth_Movement "June Tenth Movement") broke out in Keijō (Seoul) during the funeral of [King Sunjong of Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunjong_of_Korea "Sunjong of Korea").[&#8202;](#ref-back-141-0)[\[140\]](#refnote-141) Most notably, the [Gwangju(Kōshū) Students Independence Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwangju_Student_Independence_Movement "Gwangju Student Independence Movement") on 3 November 1929 led to the strengthening of Japanese military rule in 1931, after which freedom of the press and freedom of expression were curbed. Many witnesses, including Catholic priests, reported that Japanese authorities dealt with insurgency severely. When villagers were suspected of hiding rebels, entire village populations are said to have been herded into public buildings (especially churches) and massacred when the buildings were set on fire.[&#8202;](#ref-back-142-0)[\[141\]](#refnote-142) In the village of Teigan, Suigen District, Keiki Prefecture (now Jeam-ri, [Hwaseong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hwaseong_City "Hwaseong City"), Gyeonggi Province) for example, a group of 29 people were gathered inside a church which was then set afire.( [Jeamni massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeamni_massacre "Jeamni massacre"))[&#8202;](#ref-back-143-0)[\[142\]](#refnote-143)

On 10 December 1941, the Provisional Government of the Republic of Korea, under the presidency of [Kim Ku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Ku "Kim Ku"), declared war on Japan and Germany.[&#8202;](#ref-back-144-0)[\[143\]](#refnote-144)

## Independence and division of Korea

Main article: [Division of Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Division_of_Korea "Division of Korea")

Following the [dropping of atomic bombs on Hiroshima and Nagasaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_bombings_of_Hiroshima_and_Nagasaki "Atomic bombings of Hiroshima and Nagasaki"), [Soviet invasion of Manchuria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_invasion_of_Manchuria "Soviet invasion of Manchuria"), and the impending overrun of the Korean Peninsula by U.S. and Soviet forces, Japan [surrendered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surrender_of_Japan "Surrender of Japan") to the Allied forces on 15 August 1945, ending 35 years of Japanese colonial rule, though Japanese troops remained in Southern Korea for several more weeks until fully withdrawing by mid-September.[&#8202;](#ref-back-145-0)[\[144\]](#refnote-145)[&#8202;](#ref-back-146-0)[\[145\]](#refnote-146)

American forces under General [John R. Hodge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_R._Hodge "John R. Hodge") arrived at the southern part of the Korean Peninsula on 8 September 1945, while the Soviet Army and some Korean Communists had stationed themselves in the northern part of the Korean Peninsula. U.S. Colonel [Dean Rusk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dean_Rusk "Dean Rusk") proposed to Chischakov, the Soviet military administrator of northern Korea, that Korea should be split at the [38th parallel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/38th_parallel_north "38th parallel north"). This proposal was made at an emergency meeting to determine postwar [spheres of influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sphere_of_influence "Sphere of influence"), which led to the [division of Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Division_of_Korea "Division of Korea").

After the liberation of Korea from Japanese rule, the "Name Restoration Order" was issued on 23 October 1946 by the [United States Army Military Government in Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Army_Military_Government_in_Korea "United States Army Military Government in Korea") south of the [38th parallel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/38th_parallel_north "38th parallel north"), enabling Koreans to restore their names if they wished. Many [Koreans in Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koreans_in_Japan "Koreans in Japan") chose to retain their Japanese names, either to avoid discrimination, or later, to meet the requirements for naturalization as Japanese citizens.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Han-0)[\[146\]](#refnote-Han)

## Administrative divisions

Main article: [Provinces of Korea §Provinces of Chōsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provinces_of_Korea#Provinces_of_Ch%C5%8Dsen "Provinces of Korea")

|  |  |
| --- | --- |
| [![[icon]](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/Wiki_letter_w_cropped.svg/40px-Wiki_letter_w_cropped.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Wiki_letter_w_cropped.svg) | This section **needs expansion**. You can help by [adding missing information](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Korea_under_Japanese_rule&action=edit&section=). _(April 2022)_ |

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Provinces_of_Chosen_Korea.png/500px-Provinces_of_Chosen_Korea.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Provinces_of_Chosen_Korea.png) Provinces of Korea during Japanese rule

There were 13 provinces in Korea during Japanese rule: [Keiki Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keiki_Province "Keiki Province"), [Kōgen Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C5%8Dgen_Province "Kōgen Province"), [Chūseihoku Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ch%C5%ABseihoku_Province "Chūseihoku Province"), [Chūseinan Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ch%C5%ABseinan_Province "Chūseinan Province"), [Zenrahoku Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zenrahoku_Province "Zenrahoku Province"), [Zenranan Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zenranan_Province "Zenranan Province"), [Keishōhoku Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keish%C5%8Dhoku_Province "Keishōhoku Province"), [Keishōnan Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keish%C5%8Dnan_Province "Keishōnan Province"), [Heian'nan Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heian'nan_Province "Heian'nan Province"), [Heianhoku Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heianhoku_Province "Heianhoku Province"), [Kōkai Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C5%8Dkai_Province "Kōkai Province"), [Kankyōnan Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanky%C5%8Dnan_Province "Kankyōnan Province"), and [Kankyōhoku Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanky%C5%8Dhoku_Province "Kankyōhoku Province"). The administrative capital Keijō was in Keiki Province.

## Economy

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Groundbreaking_ceremony_of_Gyeongbu_Line_at_Busan%2C_1901.jpg/500px-Groundbreaking_ceremony_of_Gyeongbu_Line_at_Busan%2C_1901.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Groundbreaking_ceremony_of_Gyeongbu_Line_at_Busan,_1901.jpg) Groundbreaking ceremony for the [Keijō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keij%C5%8D "Keijō")– [Fuzan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Busan "Busan") railway[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3d/Production_in_Korea_under_Japanese_rule.png/500px-Production_in_Korea_under_Japanese_rule.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Production_in_Korea_under_Japanese_rule.png) Production in Korea under Japanese rule[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/40/Industrialization_of_Korea_under_Japanese_rule.svg/500px-Industrialization_of_Korea_under_Japanese_rule.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Industrialization_of_Korea_under_Japanese_rule.svg) Industrialization of Korea under Japanese rule[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/44/Population_of_Korea_under_Japanese_rule.png/500px-Population_of_Korea_under_Japanese_rule.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Population_of_Korea_under_Japanese_rule.png) Population of Korea under Japanese rule[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Railway_in_Korea_under_Japanese_rule.svg/500px-Railway_in_Korea_under_Japanese_rule.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Railway_in_Korea_under_Japanese_rule.svg) Km of railway in Korea under Japanese rule[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/Telephone_subscribers_in_Korea_under_Japanese_rule.svg/500px-Telephone_subscribers_in_Korea_under_Japanese_rule.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Telephone_subscribers_in_Korea_under_Japanese_rule.svg) Telephone subscribers in Korea under Japanese rule

Japan encouraged an inflow of Japanese capital to Korea's less developed economy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:022-0)[\[147\]](#refnote-:022):24 A large majority of major firms in Korea became Japanese owned and operated as a result, with key positions reserved for Japanese.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:022-1)[\[147\]](#refnote-:022):24 Koreans were permitted to work in menial roles under harsh labor conditions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:022-2)[\[147\]](#refnote-:022):24 Most of Korea's coal, iron, and crop production was shipped to Japan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:022-3)[\[147\]](#refnote-:022):24

Economic output in terms of agriculture, fishery, forestry and industry increased by tenfold from 1910 to 1945 as illustrated on the chart to the right.[&#8202;](#ref-back-GGK1942-0)[\[148\]](#refnote-GGK1942) [Princeton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princeton_University "Princeton University")'s [Atul Kohli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atul_Kohli "Atul Kohli") concluded that the economic development model the Japanese instituted played the crucial role in Korean economic development, a model that was maintained by the Koreans in the post-World War II era.[&#8202;](#ref-back-150-0)[\[149\]](#refnote-150)

Randall S. Jones wrote that "economic development during the colonial period can be said to have laid the foundation for future growth in several respects".[&#8202;](#ref-back-151-0)[\[150\]](#refnote-151)

A 2017 study found that the gradual removal of trade barriers (almost fully completed by 1923) after Japan's annexation of Korea "increased population growth rates more in the regions close to the former border between Japan and Korea than in the other regions. Furthermore, after integration, the regions close to Korea that specialized in the fabric industry, whose products were the primary goods exported from Japan to Korea, experienced more population growth than other regions close to Korea did."[&#8202;](#ref-back-152-0)[\[151\]](#refnote-152)

There were some modernization efforts by the late 19th century prior to annexation. Seoul became the first city in East Asia to have electricity, trolley cars, water, telephone, and telegraph systems all at the same time,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Summer_Institute_Summaries-0)[\[152\]](#refnote-Summer_Institute_Summaries) but Korea remained a largely backward agricultural economy around the start of the 20th century.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LoC_Economic_Development-0)[\[153\]](#refnote-LoC_Economic_Development) "Japan's initial colonial policy was to increase agricultural production in Korea to meet Japan's growing need for rice. Japan also began to build large-scale industries in Korea in the 1930s as part of the empire-wide program of economic self-sufficiency and war preparation."[&#8202;](#ref-back-LoC_Japanese_rule-0)[\[154\]](#refnote-LoC_Japanese_rule)[&#8202;](#ref-back-156-0)[\[155\]](#refnote-156) In terms of exports, "Japanese industry as a whole gained little... and this is certainly true for the most important manufacturing sector, cotton textiles. This export trade had little impact, positive or negative, on the welfare of Japanese consumer."[&#8202;](#ref-back-157-0)[\[156\]](#refnote-157) Likewise in terms of the profitability of Japanese investors: colonial Korea made no significant impact.[&#8202;](#ref-back-158-0)[\[157\]](#refnote-158)

According to scholar [Donald S. Macdonald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Stone_Macdonald "Donald Stone Macdonald"), "for centuries most Koreans lived as subsistence farmers of rice and other grains and satisfied most of their basic needs through their own labor or through barter. The manufactures of traditional Korea – principally cloth, cooking and eating utensils, furniture, jewelry, and paper – were produced by artisans in a few population centers."[&#8202;](#ref-back-LoC_Economic_Development-1)[\[153\]](#refnote-LoC_Economic_Development)

> During the early period of Japanese rule, the Japanese government attempted to completely integrate the Korean economy with Japan, and thus introduced many modern economic and social institutions and invested heavily in infrastructure, including schools, railroads and utilities. Most of these physical facilities remained in Korea after the Liberation. The Japanese government played an even more active role in developing Korea than it had played in developing the Japanese economy in the late nineteenth century. Many programs drafted in Korea in the 1920s and 1930s originated in policies drafted in Japan during the Meiji period (1868–1912). The Japanese government helped to mobilize resources for development and provided entrepreneurial leadership for these new enterprises. Colonial economic growth was initiated through powerful government efforts to expand the economic infrastructure, to increase investment in human capital through health and education and to raise productivity.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LoC_Economic_Development-2)[\[153\]](#refnote-LoC_Economic_Development)

However, under Japanese rule, many Korean resources were only used for Japan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-economic-1)[\[12\]](#refnote-economic) Economist [Suh Sang-chul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suh_Sang-chul "Suh Sang-chul") points out that the nature of industrialization during the period was as an "imposed enclave", so the impact of colonialism was trivial. Another scholar, Song Byung-nak, states that the economic condition of average Koreans deteriorated during the period despite the economic growth. Cha primarily attributed this deterioration to global economic shocks and [laissez-faire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laissez-faire "Laissez-faire") policies, as well as Chōsen's rapid population growth; the colonial government's attempts to mitigate this problem were inadequate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-shocks-1)[\[13\]](#refnote-shocks) Most Koreans at the time could access only a primary school education under restriction by the Japanese, and this prevented the growth of an indigenous entrepreneurial class. A 1939 statistic shows that among the total capital recorded by factories, about 94 percent was Japanese-owned. While Koreans owned about 61 percent of small-scale firms that had 5 to 49 employees, about 92 percent of large-scale enterprises with more than 200 employees were Japanese-owned.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Jin_W._Cyhn-0)[\[158\]](#refnote-Jin_W._Cyhn)[&#8202;](#ref-back-160-0)[\[159\]](#refnote-160)[&#8202;](#ref-back-161-0)[\[160\]](#refnote-161)

> Virtually all industries were owned either by Japan-based corporations or by Japanese corporations in Korea. As of 1942, indigenous capital constituted only 1.5 percent of the total capital invested in Korean industries. Korean entrepreneurs were charged interest rates 25 percent higher than their Japanese counterparts, so it was difficult for large Korean enterprises to emerge. More and more farmland was taken over by the Japanese, and an increasing proportion of Korean farmers either became sharecroppers or migrated to Japan or Manchuria as laborers. As greater quantities of Korean rice were exported to Japan, per capita consumption of rice among the Koreans declined; between 1932 and 1936, per capita consumption of rice declined to half the level consumed between 1912 and 1916. Although the government imported coarse grains from Manchuria to augment the Korean food supply, per capita consumption of food grains in 1944 was 35 percent below that of 1912 to 1916.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LoC_Japanese_rule-1)[\[154\]](#refnote-LoC_Japanese_rule)

In addition, 70% of the agricultural workers who made up most of Korea's population at the time were reduced to tenants of Japanese and Korean landlords who purchased land at low prices, and they had to pay high rents of 50–70%. As a result, many Koreans left for Manchuria and settled down, becoming the foundation of [Korean in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_in_China "Korean in China").[&#8202;](#ref-back-162-0)[\[161\]](#refnote-162)

After 1927, the Government-General of Korea established the "Twelve-Year Railway Plan" in discussions with the Korean Private Railway Association. In addition to the existing Hamgyŏng Line and P'yŏngwŏn Line, new routes were constructed, such as the Domun, Hyesan, Manpo, Donghae, Gyeongjeon, and Paekmu Lines.[&#8202;](#ref-back-JangChurlshin_2021-0)[\[162\]](#refnote-JangChurlshin_2021) This action aimed to facilitate the active development and efficient transportation of mineral resources distributed across northern Korea. During the Japanese colonial period, substantial progress was made in railway construction across the Korean Peninsula. Japan actively purchased privately owned railways and at the same time, established large-scale development plans. As the railroad network on the Korean Peninsula neared completion, the military railroad system intended by Japan for invasion of the continent and colonial control also took on an almost complete form.[&#8202;](#ref-back-JangChurlshin_2021-1)[\[162\]](#refnote-JangChurlshin_2021)

The railways were intended for the transportation of war materials as well as for generating economic benefits from Joseon.[&#8202;](#ref-back-JeonSungHyun_2008-0)[\[163\]](#refnote-JeonSungHyun_2008) The construction of railways was necessary for developing Korea's resource base, establishing the foundations of colonial governance, and expanding the regional economic infrastructure.[&#8202;](#ref-back-JeonSungHyun_2008-1)[\[163\]](#refnote-JeonSungHyun_2008) Government-operated railways were implemented by the Government-General of Korea, while private railways were constructed by regional private railway companies. Through the development of Korea's resources, government-operated railways aimed to expand Japan's economic benefits and to consolidate the foundations of colonial governance.[&#8202;](#ref-back-JeonSungHyun_2008-2)[\[163\]](#refnote-JeonSungHyun_2008) However, behind this initiative, there were continuous demands for railroad establishment by Japanese capitalists, centered around Japanese chambers of commerce seeking to expand economic benefits in Korea. Due to them, it was possible for the railway to be constructed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-JeonSungHyun_2008-3)[\[163\]](#refnote-JeonSungHyun_2008)

The Japanese government created a system of colonial [mercantilism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercantilism "Mercantilism"), requiring construction of significant [transportation infrastructure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_infrastructure "Transportation infrastructure") on the Korean Peninsula for the purpose of extracting and exploiting resources such as raw materials (timber), foodstuff (mostly rice and fish), and mineral resources (coal and iron ore). The Japanese developed port facilities and an extensive railway system which included a main trunk railway from the southern port city of [Pusan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Busan "Busan") through the capital of Seoul and north to the Chinese border. This infrastructure was intended not only to facilitate a colonial mercantilist economy, but was also viewed as a strategic necessity for the Japanese military to control Korea and to move large numbers of troops and materials to the Chinese border at short notice.

To transport war supplies to China, the Japanese Empire constructed railways not only from Busan to Seoul but also along the East Coast. On 1 December 1937, the Northern East Coast Line was opened with the purpose of exploiting underground resources in South and North Hamgyong Provinces and Gangwon Province to support Japanese war materials.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NamJincheon_2015-0)[\[164\]](#refnote-NamJincheon_2015) This railway connected Yangyang and Wonsan for 14 years. And the operations continued until 1950, when they were suspended due to the Korean War. This line carried passenger cars as well as approximately 15 to 20 freight cars and it was primarily used to transport timber from the Yangyang area and iron mine from local mining facilities.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NamJincheon_2015-1)[\[164\]](#refnote-NamJincheon_2015)

In the late 1920s, infrastructure that had been an obstacle to the development of Gangwon Province began to be constructed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-KoTaeWoo_2023-0)[\[165\]](#refnote-KoTaeWoo_2023) In particular, there were changes in the transportation sector. Among five new national railway lines, the Donghae Line was planned to pass through the Yeongdong region of Gangwon Province.[&#8202;](#ref-back-KoTaeWoo_2023-1)[\[165\]](#refnote-KoTaeWoo_2023) At that time, the Gangwon Provincial Office anticipated that if the entire East Coast line were completed, it would connect to Hamgyong Province, Busan, and even North Manchuria, significantly contributing to the economic development of Gangwon.[&#8202;](#ref-back-KoTaeWoo_2023-2)[\[165\]](#refnote-KoTaeWoo_2023) During the 1930s, the desire for development also increased within local communities. Residents of Gangwon actively promoted railroad construction campaigns, emphasizing the necessity of building railroads. However, as previously mentioned, these efforts were primarily driven by Japanese capitalists operating in Korea.[&#8202;](#ref-back-KoTaeWoo_2023-3)[\[165\]](#refnote-KoTaeWoo_2023)

According to materials released by the Donghae Cultural Center, economic exploitation was considerable along the eastern coast of Gangwon Province.[&#8202;](#ref-back-JeongChanghwan_2023-0)[\[166\]](#refnote-JeongChanghwan_2023) Notes remain indicating the amounts of contributions paid in each region, and there are records showing that underground resources around Samcheok were exploited and transported out of the area.[&#8202;](#ref-back-JeongChanghwan_2023-1)[\[166\]](#refnote-JeongChanghwan_2023) In 1939, an article in the Dong-A Ilbo reported that a railway was constructed to transport anthracite coal from Dokye to Mukho Port, where newly built facilities accommodated ships with a capacity of one thousand tons. At that time, photographs of Mukho Port, the Samcheok railway, and coal mines support this information.[&#8202;](#ref-back-JeongChanghwan_2023-2)[\[166\]](#refnote-JeongChanghwan_2023) These materials indicate that economic exploitation and policies to obliterate the Korean nation were also carried out along the eastern coast of Gangwon during the Japanese colonial era.[&#8202;](#ref-back-JeongChanghwan_2023-3)[\[166\]](#refnote-JeongChanghwan_2023)

Additionally, all these infrastructures were constructed by the Samcheok Development Company to transport Korean resources to Japan. Also there was the construction of the Samcheok Railway for coal transport.[&#8202;](#ref-back-JeongChanghwan_2023-4)[\[166\]](#refnote-JeongChanghwan_2023) Mukho Port was developed for the shipment of resources from the Samcheok Railway to Japan, and the company established to extract resources in this area was the Samcheok Development company.[&#8202;](#ref-back-JeongChanghwan_2023-5)[\[166\]](#refnote-JeongChanghwan_2023) It was established in April 1936 together with the [Samcheok Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samcheok_Railway "Samcheok Railway") Company. Specifically, the development of Samcheok focused on the anthracite coal fields, chemical industries that used mineral resources such as Samcheok coal like raw materials, and other enterprises related to development along the Samcheok Railway line.[&#8202;](#ref-back-KoTaeWoo′2023-0)[\[167\]](#refnote-KoTaeWoo′2023) In particular, Samcheok coal was valued for improving the Japanese Empire's coal trade balance and for coping with domestic coal demand within Japan. This was because it helped Japan reduce its dependence on coal from Manchuria, China, and French territories and supply raw materials for various industries in both Japan and Korea.[&#8202;](#ref-back-KoTaeWoo′2023-1)[\[167\]](#refnote-KoTaeWoo′2023) However, while some coal was transported to industrial areas in northern Korea, the majority was sent to areas of high demand such as Osaka, Nagoya, and Niigata in Japan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-169-0)[\[168\]](#refnote-169) All of these enterprises were established with Japanese colonial capital.[&#8202;](#ref-back-170-0)[\[169\]](#refnote-170)

In particular, the construction of railways in Hongcheon County, Gangwon Province, was led by Japanese politicians and enterprise groups. And the primary purpose is to secure economic privileges and extract resources.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ParkMihyun_2021-0)[\[170\]](#refnote-ParkMihyun_2021) During this time, independence activists carried out activities such as the explosion of railway bridges to disrupt the Japanese resource extraction network. They showed an attitude toward the railway that differed from that of Japanese capitalists and merchants.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ParkMihyun_2021-1)[\[170\]](#refnote-ParkMihyun_2021) Meanwhile, in Samcheok, which was rich in mineral resources intended for war materials, freight-only railway lines were constructed. The construction work in the railway was mobilized forced labor or the employment of workers at very low wages. Among the harsh railway laborers, there were slash-and-burn farmers and people from destitute poor who were mobilized for the work.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ParkMihyun_2021-2)[\[170\]](#refnote-ParkMihyun_2021)

Korean railways are a product of an industrialization process that was designed to enable Japan's economic exploitation of Korea.[&#8202;](#ref-back-172-0)[\[171\]](#refnote-172) Still, after the railroad network on the Korean Peninsula was established under Japanese rule, the movement of goods and human increased. Also, they had a significant impact on living environment in Korea.[&#8202;](#ref-back-173-0)[\[172\]](#refnote-173) In other words, the railway network that Japan established in Korea was primarily developed for political and military purposes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-국사편찬위원회-0)[\[173\]](#refnote-국사편찬위원회) While the railways served Japan's primary objectives of colonial governance and continental expansion, their fundamental functions of transporting food and raw materials and facilitating the export of goods and capital were also actively carried out.[&#8202;](#ref-back-국사편찬위원회-1)[\[173\]](#refnote-국사편찬위원회) Korean railways functioned to directly link the economy of colonial Korea with imperial Japan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-국사편찬위원회-2)[\[173\]](#refnote-국사편찬위원회)

From the late 1920s and into the 1930s, particularly during the tenure of Japanese Governor-General [Kazushige Ugaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazushige_Ugaki "Kazushige Ugaki"), concentrated efforts were made to build up the industrial base in Korea. This was especially true in the areas of [heavy industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heavy_industry "Heavy industry"), such as chemical plants and steel mills, and [munitions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munition "Munition") production. The Japanese military felt it would be beneficial to have production closer to the source of raw materials and closer to potential front lines for a future war with China.[&#8202;](#ref-back-175-0)[\[174\]](#refnote-175)

[Lee Young-hoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Young-hoon "Lee Young-hoon"), a professor at [Seoul National University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seoul_National_University "Seoul National University") states that less than 10% of arable land actually came under Japanese control and rice was normally traded, not robbed. He also insists that Koreans' knowledge about the era under Japanese rule is mostly made up by later educators.[&#8202;](#ref-back-176-0)[\[175\]](#refnote-176)[&#8202;](#ref-back-177-0)[\[176\]](#refnote-177)[&#8202;](#ref-back-178-0)[\[177\]](#refnote-178)[&#8202;](#ref-back-179-0)[\[178\]](#refnote-179) Many of Lee's arguments, however, have been contested.[&#8202;](#ref-back-180-0)[\[179\]](#refnote-180)

In his book, _The New Korea,_ British author [Alleyne Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alleyne_Ireland "Alleyne Ireland") referred to conditions in Korea under Japanese rule in 1926.

> Looking forward from 1910, one thing was clear where many things were obscure, namely that Japan, having decided to make Korea part of her Empire, would deem the permanence of her occupation to be a major element of her national policy, to be held intact, at whatever cost, against internal revolt or foreign intrigue.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:8-0)[\[180\]](#refnote-:8):2
>
> The Japanese refer with pride to their effective protection of life and property throughout a country but recently overrun by bandits, to the enormous increase during the past fifteen years in every branch of production, with its connotation of increased employment for Koreans, to the constantly mounting number of Koreans appointed to the government service are facts, that cannot be [gainsaid](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/gainsay "wikt:gainsay"). However, the Korean nationalists attribute to them a sinister significance.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:8-1)[\[180\]](#refnote-:8):6

### Drug trade

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/8/87/Commemorative_stamps_released_by_Japan%27s_colonial_rule_of_Korea.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Commemorative_stamps_released_by_Japan's_colonial_rule_of_Korea.jpg) 1946 saw the issue of commemorative stamps bearing the dove of peace celebrating the end of Japanese rule.

Korea produced a small amount of opium during the earlier years of the colonial period, but by the 1930s, Korea became a major exporter of both opium and narcotics, becoming a significant supplier to the illicit drug trade, specifically to the opium monopoly created by the Japanese-sponsored Manchukuo government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-0)[\[181\]](#refnote-:6)\[ _[pageneeded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources "Wikipedia:Citing sources")_\] The Government-General developed facilities dedicated to the production of morphine and heroin.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-1)[\[181\]](#refnote-:6)\[ _[pageneeded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources "Wikipedia:Citing sources")_\] Emigrant Koreans played an extensive role in drug trafficking in China, especially in [Manchuria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchuria "Manchuria"), where they were employed as poppy farmers, drug peddlers, or proprietors of opium dens – disreputable jobs that were at the bottom rung of the drug trafficking ladder.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-2)[\[181\]](#refnote-:6)\[ _[pageneeded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources "Wikipedia:Citing sources")_\] The initiation of opium and narcotic production in Korea was motivated by the worldwide shortage of opium and Japan's unfavorable environment for poppy cultivation, making the Japanese entirely dependent on foreign imports to meet domestic demand for medical opium.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-3)[\[181\]](#refnote-:6)\[ _[pageneeded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources "Wikipedia:Citing sources")_\] The Japanese discovered that Korea provided favorable climate and soil conditions for poppy cultivation; not only were the climate and soil conditions more suitable, but land and labor costs were lower than in Japan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-4)[\[181\]](#refnote-:6)\[ _[pageneeded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources "Wikipedia:Citing sources")_\] Farmers in Korea were aware of the global demand for opium, and welcomed the idea of increasing the amount of land for poppy cultivation, an idea that was introduced to them by Japanese pharmaceutical companies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-5)[\[181\]](#refnote-:6)\[ _[pageneeded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources "Wikipedia:Citing sources")_\] The sale and consumption of drugs were pervasive in Korea, where the country faced a substantial domestic drug abuse problem, appearing in the form of opium-smoking and morphine addiction.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-6)[\[181\]](#refnote-:6)\[ _[pageneeded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources "Wikipedia:Citing sources")_\] Within Korea, most illicit narcotics were supplied by Japanese druggists.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-7)[\[181\]](#refnote-:6)\[ _[pageneeded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources "Wikipedia:Citing sources")_\]

## Policies

### Motivation behind Japanese colonial policy

In Japan, attitudes toward Koreans were deeply shaped by the [Nissen dōsoron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nissen_d%C5%8Dsoron "Nissen dōsoron") (日鮮同祖論, "Theory of Japanese-Korean Common Ancestry"), which claimed Koreans and Japanese shared mythological ancestors: [Susanoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susanoo-no-Mikoto "Susanoo-no-Mikoto") (Koreans) and [Amaterasu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amaterasu "Amaterasu") (Japanese). According to this theory, Koreans were inherently Japanese. This idea served as the ideological foundation for the colonial government's assimilation policies, which aimed to fully incorporate Koreans as "racial Japanese."\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

In a speech in February 1944, Governor-General Koiso used the [Nihon Shoki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nihon_Shoki "Nihon Shoki") to justify the campaign to erase Korean language, culture, and ethnic identity. He asserted that Koreans were descendants of Susanoo-no-Mikoto, younger brother of Amaterasu, the sun goddess and ancestral figure of the Japanese emperor and nation. According to the Nihon Shoki, Susanoo had been sent to Korea to rule, a narrative Koiso used to suggest Susanoo was the same figure as [Dangun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dangun "Dangun"), Korea's mythical founder. He further argued that Korea's annexation in 1910 mirrored the "divine land transfer" ( [Kuni-yuzuri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuni-yuzuri "Kuni-yuzuri")), where Susanoo's descendant, [Ōkuninushi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%8Ckuninushi "Ōkuninushi"), submitted to [Ninigi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ninigi-no-Mikoto "Ninigi-no-Mikoto"), Amaterasu's grandson, establishing a precedent for the subordination of Koreans (Susanoo's descendants) to Japanese (Amaterasu's descendants). Koiso frequently made use of wording that hoped for the colonial policy to help "awaken" Koreans to their "true nature" as Japanese.[&#8202;](#ref-back-183-0)[\[182\]](#refnote-183)

Assimilation, however, did not mean equality. Koreans were to remain subordinate, as theorized by [Imaizumi Teisuke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imaizumi_Teisuke?action=edit&redlink=1 "Imaizumi Teisuke"), who compared the Japanese-Korean relationship to hierarchical bonds like man and woman, rider and horse. This religious ideology also explained the construction of Shinto shrines across Korea during wartime, seen as completing a divine ritual to secure Japan's destiny.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

As opposed to other occupied countries, Koreans were nominally granted Japanese citizenship, including passports, but they were denied basic rights like freedom of speech, assembly, and an independent press. By 1910, all Korean publications were banned, leaving only censored outlets run by the colonial government. Japan's ultimate goal was to erase Korean identity entirely, making Koreans indistinguishable from the Japanese, much like the assimilation policies directed at the Ainu and Okinawans. However, modern research confirms that Korean remains distinct and unrelated to Japanese or other languages.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

### Newspaper censorship

See also: [History of newspapers in Korea §Japanese colonial period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_newspapers_in_Korea#Japanese_colonial_period "History of newspapers in Korea")

In 1907, the Resident-General of Korea passed the Newspaper Act,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:2-0)[\[183\]](#refnote-:2) which effectively prevented the publication of local papers. Only the English-language _[The Korea Daily News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Korea_Daily_News "The Korea Daily News")_ and its Korean-language counterpart _Daehan Maeil Sinbo_ continued their publication, because they were run by the foreigner [Ernest Bethell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_Bethell "Ernest Bethell").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:12-0)[\[184\]](#refnote-:12)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:06-0)[\[185\]](#refnote-:06) However, after continued Japanese pressure on the paper's staff and Bethell's death in 1909, even these papers became owned by the Japanese government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:12-1)[\[184\]](#refnote-:12) The newspaper's Korean-language successor, _[Maeil Sinbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maeil_Sinbo "Maeil Sinbo")_, was subordinated to the Japanese-language _[Keijō Nippō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keij%C5%8D_Nipp%C5%8D "Keijō Nippō")._ These two papers, along with the English-language newspaper _[The Seoul Press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Seoul_Press "The Seoul Press")_, were effectively backed by the colonial government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:13-0)[\[186\]](#refnote-:13)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:22-0)[\[187\]](#refnote-:22) Concurrently, the colonial government published an [official gazette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_gazette "Government gazette"), entitled the _[Government-General of Chōsen Gazette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government-General_of_Ch%C5%8Dsen_Gazette "Government-General of Chōsen Gazette")_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:07-0)[\[188\]](#refnote-:07)

By 1910, the only major privately owned Korean newspaper allowed to publish was the _[Gyeongnam Ilbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gyeongnam_Ilbo "Gyeongnam Ilbo")_. However, this paper was pressured to close by 1915, leaving only the _Maeil Sinbo_ left. However, restrictions were eased upon the 1919 March First Movement and the [cultural rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_rule?action=edit&redlink=1 "Cultural rule")\[ [ja](https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E6%96%87%E5%8C%96%E6%94%BF%E6%B2%BB "ja:文化政治"); [ko](https://ko.wikipedia.org/wiki/%EB%AC%B8%ED%99%94%20%ED%86%B5%EC%B9%98 "ko:문화 통치")\] policy, which led to the establishment of the historic Korean papers _[The Chosun Ilbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Chosun_Ilbo "The Chosun Ilbo")_ and _[The Dong-A Ilbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Dong-A_Ilbo "The Dong-A Ilbo")_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:13-1)[\[186\]](#refnote-:13)

In 1932, Japan relaxed restrictions on the publication of Korean newspapers. Despite this, the government continued to seize newspapers without warning; there are over a thousand recorded seizures between 1920 and 1939. Revocation of publishing rights was relatively rare, and only three magazines had their rights revoked over the entire colonial period. In 1940, as the Pacific War increased in intensity, Japan shut down all Korean language newspapers again.[&#8202;](#ref-back-robinson-0)[\[189\]](#refnote-robinson)

### Education

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/57/Keijo_Imperial_University-old1.jpg/500px-Keijo_Imperial_University-old1.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Keijo_Imperial_University-old1.jpg) [Keijō Imperial University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keij%C5%8D_Imperial_University "Keijō Imperial University")[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/40/Public_primary_school_in_Korea_under_Japanese_rule-en.svg/500px-Public_primary_school_in_Korea_under_Japanese_rule-en.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Public_primary_school_in_Korea_under_Japanese_rule-en.svg) Number of public regular schools (公立普通学校) and students under Japanese rule[&#8202;](#ref-back-191-0)[\[190\]](#refnote-191)[&#8202;](#ref-back-192-0)[\[191\]](#refnote-192)[&#8202;](#ref-back-193-0)[\[192\]](#refnote-193)[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/30/Public_regular_school_enrollment_rate.svg/500px-Public_regular_school_enrollment_rate.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Public_regular_school_enrollment_rate.svg) Enrollment rate of public regular schools (公立普通学校) under Japanese rule[&#8202;](#ref-back-194-0)[\[193\]](#refnote-194)

Following the annexation of Korea, the Japanese administration introduced a public education system modeled after the [Japanese school system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_the_Empire_of_Japan "Education in the Empire of Japan") with a pyramidal hierarchy of elementary, middle and high schools, culminating at the [Keijō Imperial University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keij%C5%8D_Imperial_University "Keijō Imperial University") in [Keijō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keij%C5%8D "Keijō"). As in Japan itself, education was viewed primarily as an instrument of "the Formation of the Imperial Citizen" (황민화; 皇民化; _Kōminka_) with a heavy emphasis on moral and political instruction.[&#8202;](#ref-back-195-0)[\[194\]](#refnote-195) Japanese religious groups such as Protestant Christians willingly supported the Japanese authorities in their effort to assimilate Koreans through education.[&#8202;](#ref-back-196-0)[\[195\]](#refnote-196)

During colonial times, elementary schools were known as "Citizen Schools" (국민학교; 国民学校; _kokumin gakkō_) as in Japan, as a means of forming proper "Imperial Citizens" (황국민; 皇国民; _kōkokumin_) from early childhood. Elementary schools in South Korea today are known by the name _chodeung hakgyo_ (초등학교;初等學校) ("elementary school") as the term _gukmin hakgyo/kokumin gakkō_ has recently become a [politically incorrect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_correctness "Political correctness") term.

During the colonial period, Japan established an officially equal educational system in Korea, but it strictly limited the rate of coed education. After the Korean Educational Ordinance was published in 1938, this situation changed slightly. "Primary education consisted of a mandated four years of primary school ( _futsu gakkō_). Secondary education included four years of middle school for boys ( _koto_ _futsu_ _gakkō_) and three years for girls ( _joshi_ koto _futsu gakko_) or two to three years of vocational school ( _jitsugyo gakkō_). 1915, the Japanese announced the Regulations for Technical Schools (s _enmon gakko kisoku_), which legalized technical schools ( _senmon gakkō_) as post-secondary educational institutions."[&#8202;](#ref-back-197-0)[\[196\]](#refnote-197)

In addition, modernized (for the time) Korean educational institutions were excluded from the colonial system. 1911, Japanese government set The Regulations for Private Schools ( _Shiritsu gakko kisoku_) aimed at undermining these facilities which were believed to breed Korean patriotism.[&#8202;](#ref-back-198-0)[\[197\]](#refnote-198)

The public curriculum for most of the period was taught by Korean educators under a hybrid system focused on assimilating Koreans into the Japanese Empire while emphasizing Korean cultural education. This focused on the history of the Japanese Empire as well as inculcating reverence for the [Imperial House of Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_House_of_Japan "Imperial House of Japan") and instruction in the _[Imperial Rescript on Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Rescript_on_Education "Imperial Rescript on Education")_.

Integration of Korean students into Japanese-language schools and Japanese students in Korean-language schools was discouraged but steadily increased over time. While official policy promoted equality between ethnic Koreans and ethnic Japanese, in practice this was rarely the case.[&#8202;](#ref-back-199-0)[\[198\]](#refnote-199) Korean history and language studies would be taught side by side with Japanese history and language studies until the early 1940s under a new education ordinance that saw wartime efforts increased and the hybrid system slowly weakened.[&#8202;](#ref-back-200-0)[\[199\]](#refnote-200)

| Academic ability | Population | % |
| --- | --- | --- |
| University | 7,374 | 0.03% |
| Vocational school | 22,064 | 0.1% |
| Middle & high school | 199,642 | 0.9% |
| elementary school | 1,941,789 | 8.5% |
| temporary school & [Seodang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seodang "Seodang") | 980,122 | 4.3% |
| Uneducated | 19,642,775 | 86.2% |
| Total | 22,793,766 | 100% |

Academic Status of under Japanese rule in 1944[&#8202;](#ref-back-201-0)[\[200\]](#refnote-201)[&#8202;](#ref-back-202-0)[\[201\]](#refnote-202)

One point of view is that, although the Japanese education system in Korea was detrimental towards Korea's cultural identity, its introduction of public education as universal was a step in the right direction to improve Korea's [human capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_capital "Human capital"). Towards the end of Japanese rule, Korea saw elementary school attendance at 38 percent. Children of elite families were able to advance to higher education, while others were able to attend technical schools, allowing for "the emergence of a small but important class of well-educated white collar and technical workers... who possessed skills required to run a modern industrial economy." The Japanese education system ultimately produced hundreds of thousands of educated South Koreans who later became "the core of the postwar political and economic elite".[&#8202;](#ref-back-203-0)[\[202\]](#refnote-203)

Another point of view is that it was only after the end of Japanese rule with World War II that Korea saw true, democratic rise in public education as evidenced by the rise of adult literacy rate from 22 percent in 1945 to 87.6 percent by 1970 and 93% by the late 1980s. Though public education was made available for elementary schools during Japanese rule, Korea as a country did not experience secondary-school enrollment rates comparable to those of Japan prior to the end of World War II.[&#8202;](#ref-back-204-0)[\[203\]](#refnote-204)

For example, according to the statistics of the Japanese Government-General of Korea in 1944, which was prepared just before independence, only 8.5% of the total population entered elementary school, and the middle school enrollment rate was only 0.9%. The literacy rate was also very low, so only 30% of the population could read letters, compared to a sharp rise to 90% in 1955, 10 years after independence.[&#8202;](#ref-back-205-0)[\[204\]](#refnote-205)[&#8202;](#ref-back-206-0)[\[205\]](#refnote-206)

### Korean language

In the initial phase of Japanese rule, students were taught in Korean in public schools established by ethnic Korean officials who worked for the colonial government. Prior to this period, Korean education relied heavily on [Hanja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanja "Hanja"), Chinese characters, for written communication. However, during this time the Korean language transitioned to a [mixed Hanja–Korean script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_mixed_script "Korean mixed script") influenced by the [Japanese writing system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_writing_system "Japanese writing system"), where most lexical roots were written in Hanja and grammatical forms in Korean script.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Pratt-0)[\[206\]](#refnote-Pratt) Korean textbooks from this era included excerpts from traditional Korean stories such as [Heungbu and Nolbu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heungbu_and_Nolbu "Heungbu and Nolbu") (興夫伝; Kōfuden).[&#8202;](#ref-back-208-0)[\[207\]](#refnote-208)

In 1921, government efforts were strengthened to promote Korean media and literature throughout Korea and also in Japan. The Japanese government also created incentives to educate ethnic Japanese students in the Korean language.[&#8202;](#ref-back-209-0)[\[208\]](#refnote-209) In 1928, the [Korean Language Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Language_Society "Korean Language Society") inaugurated [Hangul Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hangul_Day "Hangul Day") (9 October), which was meant to celebrate the [Korean alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_alphabet "Korean alphabet") in the face of accelerating Japanization of Korean culture.[&#8202;](#ref-back-210-0)[\[209\]](#refnote-210)

Koreans were obliged to take Japanese names by 1932.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:022-4)[\[147\]](#refnote-:022):24

In 1933, the foundation of modern South and North Korean spelling was completed.

The Japanese administrative policy shifted more aggressively towards cultural assimilation in 1938 ( _Naisen ittai_) with a new government report advising reform to strengthen the war effort. Under this influence, in 1940, all Korean newspapers except the Japanese government's official newspaper were eliminated. In 1942, Japanese authorities caused the [Korean Language Society Incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Language_Society_Incident "Korean Language Society Incident") and closed the society by applying suspicion of rebellion to scholars.

This left less room for Korean language studies and by 1943 all Korean language courses had been phased out. Teaching and speaking of Korean was prohibited.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hopfner-0)[\[210\]](#refnote-Hopfner) Although the government report advised further, more radical reform, the 10-year plan would never fully go into effect.[&#8202;](#ref-back-212-0)[\[211\]](#refnote-212)

### Taking of historical artifacts

The Japanese rule of Korea also resulted in the relocation of tens of thousands of [cultural artifacts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_artifact "Cultural artifact") to Japan. This removal of Korean cultural property was against a long tradition of such actions dating at least since the sixteenth century wars between Korea and Japan , though in the 20th century colonial period it was a systematised and regulated activity covered by rules issued 1916–1933.[&#8202;](#ref-back-213-0)[\[212\]](#refnote-213)

The issue over where these artifacts should be located began during the U.S. [occupation of Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupation_of_Japan "Occupation of Japan").[&#8202;](#ref-back-TIME-0)[\[213\]](#refnote-TIME) In 1965, as part of the [Treaty on Basic Relations between Japan and the Republic of Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_on_Basic_Relations_between_Japan_and_the_Republic_of_Korea "Treaty on Basic Relations between Japan and the Republic of Korea"), Japan returned roughly 1,400 artifacts to Korea, and considered the diplomatic matter to have been resolved.[&#8202;](#ref-back-215-0)[\[214\]](#refnote-215) Korean artifacts are retained in the [Tōkyō National Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokyo_National_Museum "Tokyo National Museum") and in the hands of many private collectors.[&#8202;](#ref-back-newsweek-0)[\[215\]](#refnote-newsweek)

The primary building of [Gyeongbokgung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gyeongbokgung "Gyeongbokgung") Palace was demolished and the [Japanese General Government Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_General_Government_Building,_Seoul "Japanese General Government Building, Seoul") was built in its exact location. The Japanese colonial authorities destroyed 85 percent of all the buildings in Gyeongbokgung.[&#8202;](#ref-back-217-0)[\[216\]](#refnote-217) [Sungnyemun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sungnyemun "Sungnyemun"), the gate in Gyeongsong that was a symbol of Korea, was altered by the addition of large, Shinto-style golden horns near the roofs, later removed by the South Korean government after independence.

According to the South Korean government, there are 75,311 cultural artifacts that were taken from Korea. Japan has 34,369, the United States has 17,803,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Chosun_Ilbo_2006-0)[\[217\]](#refnote-Chosun_Ilbo_2006) and France had several hundred, which were seized in the [French campaign against Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_campaign_against_Korea_(1866) "French campaign against Korea (1866)") and loaned back to Korea in 2010 without an apology.[&#8202;](#ref-back-219-0)[\[218\]](#refnote-219) In 2010, Prime Minister of Japan [Naoto Kan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naoto_Kan "Naoto Kan") expressed "deep remorse" for the removal of artifacts,[&#8202;](#ref-back-220-0)[\[219\]](#refnote-220) and arranged an initial plan to return the Royal Protocols of the Joseon Dynasty and over 1,200 other books, which was carried out in 2011.[&#8202;](#ref-back-221-0)[\[220\]](#refnote-221)

### Religion

As Japan established the puppet state of [Manchukuo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchukuo "Manchukuo"), Korea became more vital to the internal communications and defense of the Japanese empire against the Soviet Union. Japan decided in the 1930s to make the Koreans become more loyal to the Emperor by requiring Korean participation in the [State Shinto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shinto_in_Korea "Shinto in Korea") devotions, and by weakening the influences of both Christianity and traditional religion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Wi_Jo_Kang_1997_62-1)[\[5\]](#refnote-Wi_Jo_Kang_1997_62)[&#8202;](#ref-back-222-0)[\[221\]](#refnote-222)

Shinto shrines were established throughout the peninsula, including [Chōsen Shrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ch%C5%8Dsen_Shrine "Chōsen Shrine") on the mountain Namsan in Seoul and [Heijō Shrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heij%C5%8D_Shrine "Heijō Shrine") in Pyongyang.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:02-0)[\[222\]](#refnote-:02)[&#8202;](#ref-back-神社참배_거부_18개교_日帝,_가차_없이_폐교{{pipe}}주간동아-0)[\[223\]](#refnote-神社참배_거부_18개교_日帝,_가차_없이_폐교{{pipe}}주간동아) Shrines such as these were destroyed shortly after Korea was liberated in 1945.[&#8202;](#ref-back-神社참배_거부_18개교_日帝,_가차_없이_폐교{{pipe}}주간동아-1)[\[223\]](#refnote-神社참배_거부_18개교_日帝,_가차_없이_폐교{{pipe}}주간동아)

#### Christianity and Communism

See also: [Christianity in Korea §Christianity under Japanese occupation, 1910–1945](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Korea#Christianity_under_Japanese_occupation,_1910%E2%80%931945 "Christianity in Korea")

See also: [Christianity in Korea §Korean nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Korea#Korean_nationalism "Christianity in Korea")

Christianity had been present in Korea in Catholic form as early as the late 18th century; Korean Catholics were subjected to several rounds of persecution by the Joseon government, culminating in the [Catholic Persecution of 1866](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Persecution_of_1866 "Catholic Persecution of 1866") (which lasted until 1872).[&#8202;](#ref-back-225-0)[\[224\]](#refnote-225) Many of those executed during these persecutions – including a number of French clerics – have since been [canonized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Martyrs "Korean Martyrs")[&#8202;](#ref-back-226-0)[\[225\]](#refnote-226) or [beatified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Martyrs#Paul_Yun_Jichung_and_123_Companions "Korean Martyrs") by the Catholic Church.[&#8202;](#ref-back-227-0)[\[226\]](#refnote-227) After the end of Joseon Korea's policy of isolation, persecution of Korean Catholics largely ceased. Protestant Christianity would not enter Korea until the late 19th century with the significant expansion of Protestant missionary activity in Asia in the 19th century. American Presbyterians and Methodists arrived in the 1880s – after the end of the Joseon government's persecution of Catholics (and the end of Joseon Korea's isolationism with treaties such as the [Joseon-United States Treaty of 1882](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseon-United_States_Treaty_of_1882 "Joseon-United States Treaty of 1882")) – and were well received. In 1914, out of 16 million people, there were 86,000 Protestants and 79,000 Catholics; by 1934 the numbers were 168,000 and 147,000. Presbyterian missionaries were especially successful.

During both the end of the Joseon era and the Japanese imperial era, harmonizing with traditional practices became an issue. Catholics, who had been persecuted during Joseon's isolation for rejecting Confucian ancestor worship practices, tolerated Shinto rites; Protestants developed a substitute for Confucian ancestral rites by merging Confucian-based and Christian death and funerary rituals.[&#8202;](#ref-back-228-0)[\[227\]](#refnote-228) During the Japanese colonial period, Christianity became an expression of Korean nationalist opposition to Japan and its assimilation policies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Danielle_Kane_2009_pp_365-1)[\[128\]](#refnote-Danielle_Kane_2009_pp_365)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Grayson-0)[\[228\]](#refnote-Grayson)

Japan began [requiring the worship of the Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Shinto "State Shinto") at the numerous Shinto shrines set up across the peninsula to bolster patriotism and national unity in 1937, which had become official Imperial government policy in Japan by 1931, complete with enforcement through persecution of traditionalist Shinto priests who did not comply.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hardacre-0)[\[229\]](#refnote-Hardacre):25[&#8202;](#ref-back-Beckford-0)[\[230\]](#refnote-Beckford):699 The [Propaganda Fide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congregation_for_the_Evangelization_of_Peoples "Congregation for the Evangelization of Peoples") of the Catholic Church permitted this practice in 1936, announcing that visits to these shrines had "only a purely civil value".[&#8202;](#ref-back-232-0)[\[231\]](#refnote-232) The Presbyterian Church in Korea did not acquiesce to this practice until 1938,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Shin2022-0)[\[232\]](#refnote-Shin2022) but was followed in this decision by other denominations such as the Methodists. Fewer than a dozen Protestant pastors refused this practice and were imprisoned. In total, around 2000 Christians were imprisoned and 50 were martyred.[&#8202;](#ref-back-EncKorCul-0)[\[233\]](#refnote-EncKorCul) The vast majority of Korean Christians followed the guidance of their denominational leaders to engage in this practice.[&#8202;](#ref-back-EncKorCul-1)[\[233\]](#refnote-EncKorCul)

Communist activity, particularly among [Korean independence activists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_independence_movement "Korean independence movement"), alarmed Christian missionaries as early as the 1920s. With the enactment of the [Peace Preservation Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_Preservation_Law "Peace Preservation Law") in 1925, communist literature was banned throughout the Empire of Japan, including Korea.[&#8202;](#ref-back-235-0)[\[234\]](#refnote-235) In order to avoid suspicion, evade this and similar statutes, and permit dissemination, Communist/socialist literature was often disguised as Christian literature addressed to missionaries. Communist concepts, such as class struggle, and its partner nationalist movement, were resonating well with some of the peasants and lower-class citizens of Korea; this was worrying to some missionaries because of communism's [atheist elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxist%E2%80%93Leninist_atheism "Marxist–Leninist atheism").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Shin2022-1)[\[232\]](#refnote-Shin2022) At one point, communist students in Seoul (named Keijō during Japanese administration) held an "anti-Sunday School conference" and loudly protested religion in front of churches. This protest renewed Japanese governmental interest in censorship of communist ideas and language.[&#8202;](#ref-back-236-0)[\[235\]](#refnote-236)

### Settlers

Under Japanese rule, the Japanese government encouraged Japanese settlers to move to Korea. As time went on and the presence of the Japanese became more established, separate segregated districts were established in cities meant for Japanese and Koreans. In Japanese districts in the cities, Japanese settlers had little contact with Koreans; while in the rural areas contact with them was more common. The Japanese tried to hold onto their lifestyle in Korea. Japanese settlers were a minority of the population in Korea. In 1943 there 758,595 Japanese people living in Korea making up 2.86% of the population.[&#8202;](#ref-back-237-0)[\[236\]](#refnote-237) For most settlers Korean was rarely spoken and mostly spoke in Japanese instead.[&#8202;](#ref-back-238-0)[\[237\]](#refnote-238)

## Legacy

### Korean diaspora

Main articles: [Zainichi Koreans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zainichi_Koreans "Zainichi Koreans"), [Sakhalin Koreans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sakhalin_Koreans "Sakhalin Koreans"), and [Koryo-saram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koryo-saram "Koryo-saram")

A number of groups in the [Korean diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_diaspora "Korean diaspora") formed because of the Japanese colonial period. Zainichi Koreans descend from the population of around 600,000 Koreans who remained in Japan, often not by choice.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:142-0)[\[238\]](#refnote-:142) After Sakhalin was transferred from Japan to the Soviet Union, most of the 43,000 ethnic Koreans there were refused permission to repatriate back to Japan or Korea, and were thus trapped in Sakhalin. Many remained stateless. They now form the Sakhalin Korean population.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Lankov-0)[\[239\]](#refnote-Lankov) Many Koreans had also escaped to Russia and the Soviet Union because of Japanese activities, and were eventually [forced to move to Central Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deportation_of_Koreans_in_the_Soviet_Union "Deportation of Koreans in the Soviet Union") in 1937.[&#8202;](#ref-back-241-0)[\[240\]](#refnote-241) They became a part of the population of Koryo-saram throughout the [former Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Soviet_states "Post-Soviet states").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:52-0)[\[241\]](#refnote-:52)

### Forced laborers, comfort women, and Unit 731

See also: [Comfort women §Legacy in South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comfort_women#Legacy_in_South_Korea "Comfort women")

During [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II "World War II"), about 450,000 Korean male laborers were involuntarily sent to Japan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-243-0)[\[242\]](#refnote-243) [Comfort women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comfort_women "Comfort women"), who served in Japanese military brothels as a form of [sexual slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_slavery "Sexual slavery"), came from all over the Japanese empire. Historical estimates range from 10,000 to 200,000, including an unknown number of Koreans. However, 200,000 is considered to be a conservative number by modern historians, and up to 500,000 comfort women are estimated to have been taken.[&#8202;](#ref-back-244-0)[\[243\]](#refnote-244)[&#8202;](#ref-back-245-0)[\[244\]](#refnote-245) These women faced an average of 29 men and up to 40 men per day, according to one surviving comfort woman.[&#8202;](#ref-back-246-0)[\[245\]](#refnote-246) However, of the 500,000, less than 50 are alive today[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Korea_under_Japanese_rule&action=edit). Comfort women were often recruited from rural locales with the promise of factory employment; business records, often from Korean subcontractees of Japanese companies, showed them falsely classified as nurses or secretaries.[&#8202;](#ref-back-247-0)[\[246\]](#refnote-247) There is evidence that the Japanese government intentionally destroyed official records regarding comfort women.[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBC_slaves-0)[\[247\]](#refnote-BBC_slaves)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Seoul_Times-0)[\[248\]](#refnote-Seoul_Times)

Koreans, along with many other Asians, were experimented on in [Unit 731](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unit_731 "Unit 731"), a secret military medical experimentation unit in World War II. The victims who died in the camp included at least 25 victims from the former Soviet Union and Korea.[&#8202;](#ref-back-People's_daily-0)[\[249\]](#refnote-People's_daily) Some historians estimate up to 250,000 total people were subjected to human experiments.[&#8202;](#ref-back-The_Guardian-0)[\[250\]](#refnote-The_Guardian) A Unit 731 veteran attested that most of those who were experimented on were Chinese, Koreans, and Mongolians.[&#8202;](#ref-back-New_York_Times-0)[\[251\]](#refnote-New_York_Times)

The Tokyo trials convened on 29 April 1946 to try Japanese personnel on Class A, B, and C charges for [crimes against peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_of_aggression "Crime of aggression"), conventional [war crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_crimes "War crimes"), and [crimes against humanity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimes_against_humanity "Crimes against humanity") respectively. Class C was created mainly to address atrocities committed by Japan against its nationals or allied citizens in times of peace or war since the [law of war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_war "Law of war") only covers enemy and neutral nationals in interstate armed conflicts.[&#8202;](#ref-back-253-0)[\[252\]](#refnote-253) However, the principal Allied powers never pressed on the Class C charges as they did not want to risk their own colonial atrocities being subject to prosecution. This left the Korean and Taiwanese victims of Japanese colonial atrocities without any recourse in the international legal system.[&#8202;](#ref-back-254-0)[\[253\]](#refnote-254)[&#8202;](#ref-back-255-0)[\[254\]](#refnote-255)

In 2002, South Korea started an investigation of Japanese collaborators. Part of the investigation was completed in 2006 and a list of names of individuals who profited from exploitation of fellow Koreans were posted.[&#8202;](#ref-back-256-0)[\[255\]](#refnote-256) The collaborators not only benefited from exploiting their countrymen, but the children of these collaborators benefited further by acquiring higher education with the exploitation money they had amassed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-257-0)[\[256\]](#refnote-257)

The "Truth Commission on Forced Mobilization under the Japanese Imperialism Republic of Korea" investigated the received reports of damage from 86 people among the 148 Koreans who were accused of being Class B and C criminals while serving as prison guards for the Japanese military during World War II. The commission, which was organized by the South Korean government, announced that they acknowledged 83 people among them as victims. The commission said that although the people reluctantly served as guards to avoid the draft, they took responsibility for the mistreatment by the Japanese against prisoners of war. Lee Se-il, leader of the investigation, said that examination of the military prosecution reports for 15 Korean prison guards, obtained from [The National Archives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_National_Archives_(United_Kingdom) "The National Archives (United Kingdom)") of the United Kingdom, confirmed that they were convicted without explicit evidence.[&#8202;](#ref-back-258-0)[\[257\]](#refnote-258)

### Korean to Japanese name change order

Although officially voluntary, and initially resisted by the Japanese Colonial Government, 80% of Koreans voluntarily changed their name to Japanese in 1940. Many community leaders urged the adoption of Japanese names to make it easy for their children to succeed in society and overcome discrimination.[&#8202;](#ref-back-259-0)[\[258\]](#refnote-259)

A study conducted by the United States [Library of Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Library_of_Congress "Library of Congress") states that "the Korean culture was quashed, and Koreans were required to speak Japanese and take Japanese names".[&#8202;](#ref-back-260-0)[\[259\]](#refnote-260)[&#8202;](#ref-back-261-0)[\[260\]](#refnote-261)[&#8202;](#ref-back-262-0)[\[261\]](#refnote-262) This name change policy, called [sōshi-kaimei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C5%8Dshi-kaimei "Sōshi-kaimei") (창씨개명; 創氏改名), was part of Japan's assimilation efforts.[&#8202;](#ref-back-263-0)[\[262\]](#refnote-263)[&#8202;](#ref-back-264-0)[\[263\]](#refnote-264) This was heavily resisted by the Korean people. Those Koreans who retained their Korean names were not allowed to enroll at school, were refused service at government offices, and were excluded from the lists for food rations and other supplies. Faced
with such compulsion, many Koreans ended up complying with the name change order. Such a radical policy was deemed to be symbolically significant in the war effort, binding the fate of Korea with that of the empire.[&#8202;](#ref-back-265-0)[\[264\]](#refnote-265)

### Discrimination against Korean leprosy patients by Japan

Colonial Korea was subject to the same _[Leprosy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leprosy "Leprosy") Prevention Laws_ of 1907 and 1931 as the Japanese home islands. These laws directly and indirectly permitted the [quarantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quarantine "Quarantine") of patients in sanitariums, where forced abortions and sterilization were common. The laws authorized punishment of patients "disturbing the peace", as most Japanese leprologists believed that vulnerability to the disease was inheritable.[&#8202;](#ref-back-266-0)[\[265\]](#refnote-266) In Korea, many leprosy patients were also subjected to hard labor.[&#8202;](#ref-back-267-0)[\[266\]](#refnote-267)
The Japanese government compensated inpatients.[&#8202;](#ref-back-268-0)[\[267\]](#refnote-268)

### Atomic bomb casualties

Main article: [Atomic bombings of Hiroshima and Nagasaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_bombings_of_Hiroshima_and_Nagasaki "Atomic bombings of Hiroshima and Nagasaki")

Many Koreans were drafted for work at military industrial factories in [Hiroshima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiroshima "Hiroshima") and [Nagasaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nagasaki "Nagasaki").[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBC_News-1)[\[92\]](#refnote-BBC_News) According to the secretary-general of a group named Peace Project Network, "there were a total of 70,000 Korean victims in both cities". Japan paid South Korea 4billion yen and built a welfare center in the name of humanitarian assistance, not as compensation to the victims.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Andreas_Hippin-1)[\[93\]](#refnote-Andreas_Hippin)

### Strong hierarchy including about age in South Korea

|  |  |
| --- | --- |
| ![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/f/f2/Edit-clear.svg/120px-Edit-clear.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) | This section may **require [cleanup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Cleanup "Wikipedia:Cleanup")** to meet Wikipedia's [quality standards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style "Wikipedia:Manual of Style"). The specific problem is: **Grammar, [MOS:OVERLINK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MOS:OVERLINK "MOS:OVERLINK"), some [WP:NPOV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:NPOV "Wikipedia:NPOV") issues, too long/detailed for a single topic.** Please help [improve this section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:EditPage/Korea_under_Japanese_rule "Special:EditPage/Korea under Japanese rule") if you can._(December 2025)__([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal "Help:Maintenance template removal"))_ |

Before the Japanese colonial rule, until the Joseon dynasty era, age hierarchy was not so severe; it was a common custom to make friends within a small age gap.[&#8202;](#ref-back-269-0)[\[268\]](#refnote-269) Today's South Korean strong age hierarchy is a remnant of the colonial rule by Japan. The [Empire of Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Japan "Empire of Japan")'s [normal schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normal_school "Normal school") ( [師範学校](https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E5%B8%AB%E7%AF%84%E5%AD%A6%E6%A0%A1 "ja:師範学校") _shihan gakkou_) are the very origin of age hierarchy in [South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Korea "South Korea").[&#8202;](#ref-back-270-0)[\[269\]](#refnote-270)

During the [Meiji Restoration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meiji_Restoration "Meiji Restoration"), the normal schools of the [Empire of Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Japan "Empire of Japan") adopted an educational method combining modern Western-style school curricula with a military-style regimen that emphasized group discipline, national loyalty, and obedience to elders, all to mold Japanese subjects who would voluntarily submit to the [Emperor of Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emperor_of_Japan "Emperor of Japan"). This synthetic educational structure entailed the discipline and supervision of junior-grade students by their seniors in a military manner. The system was imported to the Korean Peninsula when it was annexed by Japan in 1910. [Gyeongseong (Keijō) Normal School](https://ko.wikipedia.org/wiki/%EA%B2%BD%EC%84%B1%EC%82%AC%EB%B2%94%ED%95%99%EA%B5%90 "ko:경성사범학교") (경성사범학교) was one such Korean educational institution during the Japanese occupation period to adopt this military-style disciplinary system, whereby older students had the authority to monitor the behavior of younger students and even inflict corporal punishment on them. In some cases, older Korean students were even allowed to impose corporal punishment on their younger Japanese peers. Considering that Koreans suffered oppression and discrimination from the Japanese, the [Governor-General of Chōsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_Ch%C5%8Dsen "Governor-General of Chōsen"), and the Japanese government during the Japanese colonial period, this case demonstrates how powerful the age-based hierarchy was in modern Japanese schools before 1945.[&#8202;](#ref-back-271-0)[\[270\]](#refnote-271)

With the defeat of the [Empire of Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Japan "Empire of Japan") in 1945 and the establishment of the [US military headquarters (GHQ)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Commander_for_the_Allied_Powers "Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers") in Japan, the disbandment of the Japanese military, and the comprehensive overhaul of the Japanese educational system, hierarchical order in Japanese society weakened, and age-based hierarchy became mild or disappeared.[&#8202;](#ref-back-272-0)[\[271\]](#refnote-272)[&#8202;](#ref-back-273-0)[\[272\]](#refnote-273)

However, since [Park Chung-hee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Park_Chung-hee "Park Chung-hee"), who served in the military of [Manchukuo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchukuo "Manchukuo"), a puppet state of the [Empire of Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Japan "Empire of Japan"), became president, South Korea has reinforced the severe age hierarchy that has been carried over from Japanese rule. For example, the Park Chung-hee administration published and disseminated the [National Charter of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Charter_of_Education?action=edit&redlink=1 "National Charter of Education") (국민교육헌장), which benchmarked the [Imperial Rescript on Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Rescript_on_Education "Imperial Rescript on Education") of the [Empire of Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Japan "Empire of Japan"). This, combined with the Confucian tradition of venerating elders, has led to the current severe age hierarchy and other bad hierarchical practices in [South Korean society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Korean_society "South Korean society").[&#8202;](#ref-back-274-0)[\[273\]](#refnote-274)[&#8202;](#ref-back-275-0)[\[274\]](#refnote-275)

This bad hierarchical system, imported during the Japanese colonial period and maintained and strengthened by [Park Chung-hee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Park_Chung-hee "Park Chung-hee"), affected not only the age hierarchy but also middle school, high school, university, and work culture. As a representative example, in middle and high schools, there is one thing where students are asked to form a military-style line.[&#8202;](#ref-back-276-0)[\[275\]](#refnote-276) When living in society, there are bosses who force military-style language at work. In fact, the South Korean military has already abolished this military-style language.[&#8202;](#ref-back-277-0)[\[276\]](#refnote-277)

### South Korean presidential investigation commission on pro-Japanese collaborators
